# process

## playtesting practice
- [model] Testing types answer different questions: internal reviews discuss design, QA finds bugs, focus groups rank features, gauge purchase intent and marketing appeal, usability checks interfaces, playtesting checks intended player experience. (x2)
- [model] Each playtest is framed by six probing questions covering purpose, participants, timing, setting, focus and method; purpose comes first because the rest depend on it.
- [model] A session observation form has three parts: in-game observations and questions, postgame questions, and revision ideas.
- [model] Playtesting can be split into discrete phases, each focused on specific design aspects and perfected before advancing, moving the game from core prototype to complete model. When: When moving from a core gameplay prototype toward a fully functioning, balanced game model. Why: Each phase keeps the designer in control of revision.
- [model] A branch test plan table lists each quest step as a row, with columns for implementation, dialogue, task, reward, and balancing, and marks each cell OK, BUG, N/A, or TODO. When: testing a branching quest.
- [definition] A playthrough test has players play most or all of a game to find engageability problems, such as difficulty spikes and goals that stop being exciting.
- [definition] One-on-one testing observes a player directly, with notes taken and questions asked before and after play; it is the norm in professional usability testing.
- [definition] An interview is a face-to-face, in-depth oral session after play, closer to a verbal quiz than a discussion.
- [definition] Playtesting checks whether the game delivers the designer's intended player experience goals; it is an ongoing designer activity.
- [definition] A closed beta is a nearly finished game tested from home by a few thousand players, often answering online surveys.
- [definition] Kleenex testing uses people who have never played the game, reveals reactions in the first critical moments, and uses each tester only once.
- [definition] Over-the-shoulder testing means watching others play, either informally beside a coworker or in a staged room with cameras.
- [definition] A playtest protocol lays out how a session is run, intended to show how the design performs in practice.
- [definition] Focus testing is market research in which participants discuss product ideas in a group, and it does not require a working game.
- [definition] A task analysis test checks whether players can complete one specific task, such as building a deck or navigating a map, measured by completion time and number of errors.
- [definition] Confidants are friends and colleagues outside the design team who notice things the designer has missed.
- [definition] A closed beta is invite-only, while an open beta lets anyone sign up.
- [definition] Probability predicts outcomes from a known random process, while statistics infers the process that produced observed data.
- [definition] A testing plan documents every way to play through content, so that every branch is tested.
- [definition] LTV is the revenue from a player between first play and quitting, and it sets the ceiling on acquisition budget per user.
- [mechanism] In live-service titles, players expect patches and new content after launch, so shipping acts as the largest playtest. When: Games delivered as ongoing services. Why: Post-launch player behavior and expectations reveal problems that pre-release testing missed.
- [mechanism] After roughly 10 to 20 playtests, a designer shifts from thinking in single stories to thinking in systems, and can predict how a change ripples through the whole game. Why: Many observations combine into a model of cause and effect.
- [mechanism] A rule change on a paper prototype can be tested almost at once, for example by swapping a turn structure and replaying. When: When testing core game mechanics with a physical paper prototype. Why: A rule change only requires rewriting the rule and replaying, with no implementation work needed.
- [mechanism] Studies with non-rejected null hypotheses are often not submitted or accepted, which biases the published record toward confirmation and hides potentially useful findings. When: When a study fails to reject its null hypothesis. Why: Unpublished negative results push researchers toward confirming bias and remove findings others could build on.
- [mechanism] Playthrough tests in alpha reveal how players progress and where engagement drops, and can expose missing telemetry hooks. When: Alpha, once the game is feature complete or sooner. Why: Observed play exposes which tracking points are absent and need adding.
- [mechanism] Focus groups surface common wisdom and key disagreements, and group interaction pushes people to sharpen their own ideas. When: When generating ideas with a group. Why: Differences of opinion prompt people to refine positions and try alternatives.
- [mechanism] Involving QA early makes them more invested and more likely to prioritize the game during crunch time. Why: Shared investment carries into crunch, when staff are willing to work the extra hours needed.
- [mechanism] When one column has values far smaller than the others, its line appears flat along the bottom of a shared chart. Charting that column alone reveals its real shape. When: Plotting several columns with very different magnitudes on one chart. Why: The shared vertical axis is scaled to the largest values, compressing the small ones.
- [behavior] A playtest exposes decisions the team felt sure about but got wrong, which is why it feels uncomfortable. Why: Testers' criticism lands on work the designer is attached to, so being shown wrong is painful.
- [behavior] Memory favors expected or wanted comments, so written notes are needed to preserve important surprises. When: after a playtest session. Why: recall is biased toward what was anticipated.
- [behavior] Once a game is playable, the whole team, including artists, designers, and programmers, plays it informally, and excessive team play signals that the game is fun. When: Game is playable, before beta. Why: The team's own gaming enthusiasm means they play spontaneously, so unprompted play reveals enjoyment.
- [behavior] Watching testers get bored, hit a difficulty spike or find a game-breaking bug motivates developers to fix the design. Why: Playtest results are real consequences, not rewards someone else sets up, so they don't feel controlling.
- [behavior] Expert gamers compare a game to its genre using technical terms and specific examples, but they are often jaded and prefer complex challenges that only a small share of the public shares. When: When choosing expert playtesters. Why: Experienced players have played many similar games and hold demanding tastes.
- [behavior] Fresh players who have never seen the game notice things the team has grown used to and are suited to usability, communication, and first-impression questions, but they cannot reveal boredom that appears over many sessions. When: When choosing playtesters for a given question. Why: Fresh players have no prior exposure, so they judge first impressions and miss long-term fatigue.
- [behavior] Remarks such as 'the rules don't say either way,' 'I'm completely stuck,' and 'you can't do that!' signal rule gaps. When: Observing playtests. Why: They reveal unclear rules, dead ends, and unclear permissions.
- [behavior] A bad client suggestion usually means the client is trying to help and reacting to an unstated problem, not that they are foolish.
- [behavior] Input quality is hard to evaluate because the work is hidden; players credit graphics or balance for successes actually due to good input. When: When a game's input system is very good, so its quality is hard to see. Why: Good input looks effortless, so its contribution goes unseen.
- [behavior] The first outside playtest of a fully planned game typically exposes degenerate strategies, missed story beats, and unlearned mechanics that the plan did not anticipate. When: at the first outside playtest. Why: The designer's plan fixes predetermined roles for every piece, and outside players act outside that plan.
- [behavior] A leader who watches one review can feel they fully understand a design, though it may play out in thousands of different ways. When: A leader judging a design from a single review session. Why: What is seen in one session feels like the whole picture.
- [behavior] Some players freeze or fall silent when they must play and talk at once, which is exactly when insight is most valuable. When: during think-aloud sessions. Why: Dividing attention between playing and speaking overloads some players, who stop talking when gameplay gets stressful.
- [behavior] Players may react to intended content with an unintended emotion, so their response can differ from the designed tone.
- [behavior] A tester new to the game exposes the obstacles that will trip up first-time players. When: When testing with players who are new to games or to the genre.
- [behavior] When challenge and skill fall out of balance, players become bored or frustrated and may stop playing. When: As a player's perceived skill changes over the course of a game.
- [behavior] Testers who do not regularly play your type of game give less useful feedback. When: Choosing testers for a genre. Why: Genre players can weigh the game against titles they already know, adding market research.
- [behavior] When players cannot heal themselves and must rely on others, they tend to ask for help and assist each other, even though designers may fear this frustrates players. When: When a designer removes a player's ability to fully succeed alone. Why: Dependence on others prompts requests for help and mutual assistance.
- [behavior] Talking aloud makes participants focus more than usual, so they work out the system more than they would alone. When: Running think-aloud tests. Why: Verbalizing reasoning increases attention to the task.
- [behavior] Almost every card-mix decision meets strong opposition from a segment of players.
- [behavior] Testers who want to please the host say what they think the designer wants to hear, and refusing criticism makes this more likely. When: the designer rejects criticism. Why: Testers who fear displeasing the host tailor their answers.
- [behavior] A vocal tester can sway the whole group, which is why professional labs isolate testers from each other.
- [behavior] A vague note using a word like awesome usually means the commenter wanted to feel strong and in control, and the scene did not deliver that. When: When feedback on writing comes from non-writers who lack the jargon.
- [behavior] Players who know the real sport notice when a sports game lacks tried and tested plays. When: a sports game uses invented or missing plays. Why: fans of the real sport know the authentic plays.
- [behavior] Players often cannot tell what their actions will teach, because feedback spreads across many input-output pairs and the network generalizes. When: Players teaching a neural network creature through feedback. Why: Neural networks learn by generalizing, so feedback is distributed over several actions.
- [behavior] People recognize well-designed things through experience even when they cannot name the qualities that make them good. When: Judging whether a space, object, or game feels right. Why: The nameless quality is felt rather than articulated.
- [behavior] Players learn most by pushing simulations until they break, and seeing why a failure occurs builds insight into the whole system. When: when failure is allowed in a simulation. Why: Failures reveal how the parts of the system interact.
- [behavior] Many people believe they understand game design because they experience games as players and humans, so their feedback can seem expert. When: when non-designers give design feedback. Why: Personal experience as a user feels like design expertise.
- [behavior] Playtesters found a flat 2D view of a flying game unemotional, and a 3D world locked to a 2D plane conflicted between clarity and freedom. When: prototyping a flight game with an emotional goal. Why: A restricted view limits the sense of freedom that players value.
- [behavior] Players were astonished that machine-generated titles could be so simple and fun, and designers accepted such games while they stayed lightweight. When: when players first encounter machine-generated games. Why: Simple, obvious rules were unexpected from a generator.
- [behavior] Players may harm sympathetic characters purely to test whether a mechanic works, not out of hostility toward the character. When: a mechanic is untested or its outcome is uncertain. Why: curiosity about mechanics outweighs the character's relationships.
- [behavior] Problems such as degenerate strategies often appear only after skilled players test a system. When: When a system's flaws stay hidden beneath its surface appeal.
- [behavior] Designer intuition for balance improves with practice when each exact guess is tested and the result informs the next guess. When: When tuning values by feel over many iterations. Why: Each test provides data that sharpens the next judgment.
- [rule] Record screen, face and hands together with several cameras in one split-screen image; observe what players see, feel, and whether controls work as expected; review when time allows. When: When there is time to review recordings after sessions. Why: Seeing screen, face and hands together shows how the three interrelate. (x2)
- [rule] Testers should report problems and experiences, not design solutions: ask which parts felt boring rather than for difficulty changes; the designer chooses and builds the fix. Why: Most testers who are not designers lack the experience to diagnose a problem and pick the best fix. (x2)
- [rule] Recruit playtesters similar to the target market, diverse across that audience rather than a narrow slice, and as many as possible. When: When collecting playtest data for a game with a defined target market. Why: Representative and sufficiently large samples filter out random variation and bias. (x2)
- [rule] Give help only when testers are truly stuck and record where and why; hints hide the flaw and turn the test into a test of a different game. (x2)
- [rule] Answer a tester's question with a question, such as asking what they think they should do; repeat their last words when they trail off rather than finishing them. When: Moderating a think-aloud session. Why: Completing a participant's thoughts biases what they report. (x2)
- [rule] Test early: watching a few playtesters is most valuable on a rough prototype, because changes become too costly to make meaningfully later. When: early prototype stage. Why: Core-mechanic changes would discard earlier balance work, and recruiting enough observed testers becomes impractical near launch. (x2)
- [rule] Recruit outside players who did not take part in the design to extend testing beyond the team; without in-house QA, arrange them as regular testers. When: When a studio has no in-house QA department. (x2)
- [rule] Playtest when useful and at least weekly; testing only when convenient causes long feedback gaps where problems accumulate unseen. Why: A fixed minimum frequency turns playtesting into a routine habit and prevents long feedback gaps.
- [rule] Choose the playtest venue from the questions it must answer, since each venue trades convenience, comfort, cost, realism, and reach. Why: Venues differ in cost, control over conditions and how many testers they reach.
- [rule] Help testers in unfamiliar surroundings get comfortable, for example by inviting friends. When: testing at a studio or lab. Why: Strangers in an unfamiliar room may hold back from playing freely or voicing their opinions.
- [rule] Hold group interviews after private ones, to see whether friends reveal new information together. When: When the tester has other friends who are also testing the game. Why: Friends talking together can surface new information, but people speak less candidly when acquaintances are listening, so the private interview comes first.
- [rule] Avoid asking about specific moments after play; players forget details irrelevant to their goal, so ask about those during play. Why: Players focused on the goal often do not form memories of details unrelated to it.
- [rule] Give every planned question a way to produce an answer.
- [rule] Keep bringing in new playtesters, because long-term testers become experts and cannot judge the true difficulty of the easiest mode. When: When the same playtesters are used throughout development. Why: Experienced testers lose the novice viewpoint, while newcomers show how beginners actually react to the easiest mode.
- [rule] At the first playable stage, test with a small pool such as the dev team, trusted friends under NDA, or a designer's local playtest group. When: first playable stage. Why: The build is rough and unannounced, so testing stays with a small trusted group and is not yet about balance.
- [rule] Do not let griefer testers advocate for changes, but test with them to learn what players can do to each other and where boundaries are needed. Why: Aggressive testers reveal the major exploits that otherwise block all other playtesting.
- [rule] Have testers take every spiteful or self-serving action that gives even a slight advantage, short of cheating, and refuse cooperation unless it pays materially. When: checking for incentives that reward harming others. Why: A dominant strategy bores its user and frustrates opponents, so players who exploit it erode the fun.
- [rule] Tell testers what is being tested and how to approach it, unless secrecy is needed for valid results, since vague requests leave them unsure what to do. When: When secrecy is not needed for a valid test result.
- [rule] Ask programmers for test cheats, since many shipped cheats began as aids that sped up testing. When: When testing a specific element in a video game that must occur on cue. Why: Forcing a specific scenario is the only way to ensure the tested element actually occurs during the test.
- [rule] Observe real players when judging a design, because a game system's effect is measured by the experience it creates for those players. When: When judging a design that is still in progress. Why: Experience is what a system produces, and only live players reveal that experience.
- [rule] Invite players who match the target audience and do not know you personally, since friends, family and teammates are too close to the game. Why: Designers' familiarity blinds them to how newcomers with different mental models perceive the game.
- [rule] Find usability problems by observing what players actually do, rather than relying on their explanations or social media complaints about the game. When: when diagnosing why players are frustrated. Why: Players often feel frustrated without knowing the cause and may misattribute it, which can send the team to the wrong fix.
- [rule] In playthrough playtests, let players play without guidance, as they would at home, so their unassisted behavior is what gets observed. Why: Players often misattribute the source of their frustration, so watching their unguided actions identifies the real cause more accurately.
- [rule] Test key visual and audio designs with players, because perception varies between people and designers cannot judge it objectively. Why: Designers suffer from the curse of knowledge.
- [rule] Run playthrough studies with bigger participant pools so results reflect differences between players, including those who have impairments. Why: Larger groups capture the range of player differences, including players with disabilities.
- [rule] Limit each moderator to observing two players at once, because multitasking makes observation unreliable.
- [rule] During sessions with testers, record where their eyes travel, where they click or move while stuck, annoyed or disengaged, and capture every remark they make. Why: Designers lose objectivity after long iteration, so testers' behavior offers a fresh view of the game.
- [rule] Playtest throughout development with the designer, the team, office visitors and groups matching the target audience. Why: Experience cannot be fully predicted, so decisions must rest on how the in-progress prototype actually plays.
- [rule] Do not skip playtesting because your setup is imperfect; adapt the method to your environment. When: When the prototype or environment rules out a perfect testing setup.
- [rule] Record every playtest session with its date, feedback and observations in a chronological record.
- [rule] During play, record your own thoughts and questions, such as why a choice was made or whether a rule seems confusing.
- [rule] Disclose any recording or observers at the start of a playtest and assure testers that recordings stay within the design team. When: Starting a playtest session.
- [rule] Write a session script covering your open questions, phrased so it does not lead testers. When: Preparing a playtest session. Why: A written script keeps the designer observing rather than steering.
- [rule] Listen to testers and analyze what they say so you see mechanics as they actually are, not as you want or imagine them to be. When: Reviewing playtest feedback. Why: Grasping what was actually built, not what was intended, is what lets a designer refine the game gradually over time.
- [rule] Make sure the game works as intended, is internally complete, balanced, and fun. When: Setting priorities during testing. Why: Teams working long stretches on a project tend to lose track of the player's perspective, which the designer must keep representing.
- [rule] Do not lead testers; when they ask what to do, ask them what they think they should do. When: Testers ask for guidance during a session. Why: Answering directly steers behavior and hides how the design works unaided.
- [rule] Give the prototype to people who have never played it, give no verbal instructions beyond the challenge to play, and watch whether they reach the end unaided. When: Checking whether a prototype is functional. Why: Completeness can only be judged once a newcomer can progress unaided, since a non-functional system blocks that judgment.
- [rule] Stay open, neutral and professional; ask in a wondering, not accusatory, way; do not reward 'right' answers; show gratitude, since testers are doing a favor. When: during playtest questioning. Why: Outward reactions from the designer make testers stop answering candidly, and rewarding answers pushes them toward pleasing the interviewer.
- [rule] Recruit testers who differ in skill, age, gender, temperament, culture, socioeconomic background, interests and game knowledge, do not know the game like the designer, and resemble the final players. When: When playtesting by watching other players rather than testing the game alone. Why: Testers who vary and lack the designer's knowledge respond more like real players.
- [rule] Use external participants whenever available; internal testers see games differently and may know the designer, heavily biasing their reactions. When: When external participants are not yet available, before formal UX tests can be run. Why: Internal testing is only a stopgap; familiarity and insider knowledge distort reactions.
- [rule] Observers stay neutral and silent, showing no laughter, sighs or cheering, and developers wait for the report rather than judging early. When: Observing a UX test. Why: Visible reactions and early judgments can steer participants and distort analysis.
- [rule] Games with goals beyond fun, such as teaching or activism, need different test goals than fun. When: Game aims at non-entertainment outcomes. Why: Fun-focused criteria measure what keeps voluntary players engaged, so a game aiming at other outcomes must be measured against those outcomes instead.
- [rule] Find starvation problems through playtesting before release, when they can still be fixed, because they are always found eventually. When: When a FAQ or explanatory text noticeably improves the game, suggesting missing information. Why: Designers know far more than players, so a gap in the information players receive stays invisible to them without testing.
- [rule] Playtests should reveal what players experience, not just collect suggestions, because designers are too close to the game to feel what another player feels. Why: Designers lack the outside perspective needed to feel what another player feels, so watching testers play reveals it.
- [rule] Observe many sessions to map the experiences a game can produce, including its branch points and how they interact. When: When a game offers many possible paths and playtesters take different threads through it. Why: A single path cannot reveal interactions between branches.
- [rule] Stop running playtests when testers repeatedly show the same experiences, since the designer then understands enough to decide. Why: Once testers repeat experiences, the designer can predict how changes will affect the game as a system.
- [rule] Avoid leading questions such as asking whether a player noticed a specific object, because players answer yes to appear perceptive or to please the interviewer. When: When checking whether a player perceived a particular element of the game. Why: The question itself hands the tester information, so they answer yes to seem perceptive or to please the interviewer.
- [rule] When players genuinely need information the game lacks, deliver it through a method planned in advance as part of the test protocol. Why: Unplanned hints from the designer would contaminate the test by revealing knowledge testers should not hold.
- [rule] Playtest at every development stage, not only at beta, because waiting leaves fundamental design issues too late to fix. When: When the game is still before its beta stage, while assets are not yet complete. Why: late discovery of core problems is costly.
- [rule] Choose non-digital or digital test forms according to the development stage. When: choosing how to test a design. Why: early stages can be tested before digital work exists.
- [rule] Prepare focused questions or a paper survey before a playtest, and record extra comments while holding to the test's goals. When: planning a playtest. Why: focus keeps results useful.
- [rule] Vet enthusiastic player suggestions against the game's core vision, and dismiss them kindly if they do not fit. When: players offer design suggestions. Why: a coherent vision matters more than any single idea.
- [rule] Match each tester group to the questions you need answered, and use a mix of expert and fresh testers at different times, since most games need both. When: When planning playtest sessions. Why: Each tester group reveals different kinds of problems.
- [rule] Log important game events during each session, such as time spent in character creation, hits needed to defeat a boss, average score, and most-used weapons. When: When evaluating player behavior during playtests or live play. Why: Logged events reveal how players actually play.
- [rule] Phrase suggestions as options for the team to weigh rather than as orders, since the researcher clarifies problems and does not take control of design. When: Writing fix suggestions in a UX report. Why: Directive wording makes the report feel bossy and reduces its usefulness.
- [rule] Play the build first and prepare an observation sheet with specific objectives; give feedback on features ready for testing, not unfinished ones. When: Preparing for a UX test. Why: Developers dislike feedback on incomplete work, and objectives focus observation.
- [rule] Participants must not watch each other play, to avoid peer pressure and copying. When: Running group or sequential sessions. Why: Watching others changes how a participant behaves.
- [rule] Tell participants the game is being tested rather than their skill, that mild confusion is worth reporting, and that researchers do not work on the game so harsh feedback will not hurt anyone. When: Briefing participants before a test. Why: Framing reduces politeness bias and encourages honest criticism.
- [rule] Participants sign an NDA, and recording devices are stored in a locker whose key the participant keeps. When: Running a UX test with external participants. Why: Protects confidentiality and recordings.
- [rule] Run task analysis sessions one-on-one, with one participant observed by one researcher.
- [rule] Build a studio-wide UX strategy so that user experience becomes everyone's concern. Why: A shared framework gives separate teams one set of player-experience criteria to guide iteration, research, and analytics.
- [rule] Do not answer criticism; write it down and listen carefully. Why: Designers are attached to what they made and tend to get defensive, which shuts down useful feedback.
- [rule] Aim playtests to discover what players dislike or misunderstand, not to collect praise. Why: Praise reveals nothing about what needs fixing, so the goal is to surface what testers dislike or misunderstand.
- [rule] Set ground rules at the start of group sessions: everyone respects opinions, everyone gets a chance to speak, and no one criticizes another tester's ideas. When: When several testers are questioned together in one group session. Why: A particularly vocal tester can sway the others, so clear etiquette keeps each person's opinions independent.
- [rule] Observe testers from behind one-way glass, through a video feed, or quietly from inside the room. When: Running a playtest session of a game that is still in development. Why: Quietly watching testers yields more learning about how they actually play than guiding them does.
- [rule] Observe confidant testers without asking leading questions. When: Testing a prototype with friends or family.
- [rule] Playtest your own prototype and record your thoughts in a playtesting notebook. When: Early prototype stage, when the goal is making the game work at rough quality. Why: Self-play is how core mechanics are found and glaring problems get solved at this stage.
- [rule] For most tests, recruit new testers for fresh input; later, bring back articulate testers to see progress and whether removed features were missed. When: Later in the design process, when checking how the game has progressed. Why: Returning testers can point out whether removed or changed features no longer work as well.
- [rule] Test regularly with target-audience participants who know nothing about the game, not friends or family, who please the team with soft feedback; developers cannot see their own knowledge gaps. When: Regularly during development, with testers from the target audience who know nothing about the game. Why: Developers cannot perceive their own knowledge gaps; biased testers distort conclusions.
- [rule] Before building a complex choice, check with QA testers whether a simpler one looks the same to players.
- [rule] When humor derails a group discussion, the facilitator must steer it back to the topic. When: Brainstorming sessions that include jokes. Why: Unchecked jokes can take over the session.
- [rule] Log every playtest fix in writing, because an unrecorded patch tends to resurface later. When: during playtesting.
- [rule] Test interface sounds with other people to check that each one reads as its intended meaning. When: Choosing UI feedback sounds such as forward, back, positive, or negative cues.
- [rule] Do not graph a single value, whether discrete or continuous; state the number directly. When: Presenting one analytics value at a single point in time.
- [rule] Plot each metric as a time series and mark significant patch dates on the graph. When: Tracking a live game's metrics across updates. Why: Averages hide change, while time plots reveal when shifts happened.
- [rule] Build a small representative set of content for playtesting, such as three roles or one baseline character, rather than full content. When: early in content creation. Why: keeps early builds playable and cheap to change.
- [rule] When a group is underrepresented, each candidate cause implies a different fix, so test the causes separately before choosing an intervention. When: investigating a gap in applicants or hires. Why: Different causes such as outreach, wording, bias, offers or chance require different responses.
- [rule] Test reward strategies with players, since predicting which rewards will hamper intrinsic motivation is difficult. When: choosing rewards. Why: intuition about reward effects is often wrong.
- [rule] Use standardized protocols to test hypotheses, because biases affect developers and players alike. Why: Biases affect developers and players alike, and without standard procedures it is easy to miss or misread what happens.
- [rule] Write test questions that match the specific feedback you need. When: When content is ready for testing and you know what feedback you need. Why: Premature content or unfocused questions produce random feedback you do not need.
- [rule] Test against predefined UX goals, pillars, and metrics, and guard analysis against cognitive biases. When: During the testing step of an iterative development cycle. Why: Cognitive biases and hasty conclusions distort analysis unless they are controlled for.
- [rule] Test VR with a wide range of physical attributes, not only typical player segments, since body size and mobility issues are missed otherwise. When: Developing VR experiences where the player's avatar matches their real-world height. Why: Body size changes how experience design works, so typical player segments miss edge cases and feelings of power.
- [rule] Do not treat a single small test as proof that a design change works. When: interpreting playtest results. Why: Single studies can be wrong or later discredited.
- [rule] With a dedicated UX team, use an integrated UX process to plan what needs testing and when. When: Dedicated UX team exists.
- [rule] Hold editing sessions on different days from brainstorming so ideas mature; mixing the two reduces the productivity of both. Why: Letting time pass between phases keeps evaluation from blurring into brainstorming, which lowers brainstorming's output.
- [rule] Use focus groups to generate ideas, not to judge the quality or popularity of an idea. When: Running a focus group where attendees are invited to talk openly and push back. Why: Group dynamics distort judgments of ideas.
- [rule] Accessibility testers must belong to the target market, be objective rather than friends or relatives, and have never played the game; earlier playtesters are disqualified. When: recruiting accessibility testers. Why: Prior exposure or personal ties bias what testers notice and tolerate.
- [rule] Treat participant struggles as valid evidence; a designer's opinion that a feature is obvious does not outweigh observed confusion. When: interpreting accessibility test results. Why: Observed confusion reflects the actual experience of new players.
- [rule] Give remote playtesters a channel to talk to each other so social and competitive exchange can still surface. When: remote playtests. Why: Room cues are lost when testers are apart.
- [rule] Test whether an audience wants freedom to set its own goals before adding open-ended personal goal setting. When: Deciding whether to add open-ended personal goal setting for an audience that may find open systems too open-ended. Why: Players set private subgoals within systems they cannot win, seeking a sense of accomplishment anyway.
- [rule] Route requests through the lead or technical director rather than going around them, because bypassing them undercuts their authority. When: Working in a team with a programming hierarchy, whether large or small.
- [rule] Send change requests to the owner of the decision, such as the art director rather than the person who would carry it out, and consult the technical director before changing the database or interface. When: When a change needs to touch the database or part of the interface.
- [rule] Use geopolitical review to catch cultural problems before release; handling sensitive themes is a judgment about audience and region, not censorship. When: Large games whose sensitive themes must suit the intended audience and region of distribution. Why: Deep localization reveals regional taboos and how in-game context can strengthen or soften a message's meaning for players.
- [rule] Agree with the client on what player experience to measure in beta testing and which questions to ask beta testers.
- [rule] Ask reviewers to sign a non-disclosure agreement to protect your ideas, since industry people are used to signing them. When: you worry about idea theft. Why: A confidentiality commitment deters outside reviewers from taking shared concepts for their own use.
- [rule] Discuss how reviewers' ideas could improve the design, but make the final decision yourself.
- [rule] Thank every reviewer, whether or not you plan to use their comment.
- [rule] In each test row, note what key information the step conveys, how it fits the story, and any design concerns such as weak side quests or overly hard encounters. When: recording test results.
- [rule] In a party-based RPG, test party compositions that lack key abilities, such as no lockpicker or no healing magic, and teams made only of one class. When: testing a party-based RPG.
- [rule] Validate designs with real users instead of assuming they work, because perception is subjective. Why: The player's experience forms in their minds rather than being contained in the design, so the designer's intent alone cannot reveal how it lands.
- [rule] After each match, highlight one standout moment and show personal statistics compared with the player's past performance, so players feel good even on a losing team. When: After a match that the player's team does not win. Why: positive feedback on individual performance offsets team loss.
- [rule] Study other games' cost curves for insight rather than copying their math. When: Researching balance in comparable games. Why: Each game's systems differ, so numbers borrowed from another game will probably misfit this design.
- [rule] To test whether a balance model finds the most broken objects, pick clearly overpowered, clearly underpowered and well-balanced objects, focusing on extremes rather than slightly off ones.
- [rule] Simplifying a simulation by removing player choices, such as fixed buying policies, limits what it can reveal about the game's balance. When: when building a simulation for balance analysis. Why: Fixed policies remove the decisions that shape real outcomes.
- [rule] Repeat a shuffle simulation many times and average the results, because a single trial gives no meaningful average.
- [rule] Balance with structured, systematic approaches, because haphazard changes make the game swing between imbalanced states. Why: Mechanics are linked through many strategies, so each fix to one ripples into new imbalances elsewhere.
- [heuristic] Playtest at every development stage, from pre-idea to shipped, starting as early as possible; 'the game isn't ready' should not delay testing. Why: Problems are discovered earlier, and a design becomes harder to change the longer it is held back. (x2)
- [heuristic] Watching playtest video can reveal play styles that score data alone does not show. Why: Video shows the behavior behind score gaps that the numbers alone cannot explain.
- [heuristic] Playtest with both novices and experienced players, since insiders alone frustrate newcomers and newcomers alone bore experts. Why: Each group exposes different failure points in the game.
- [heuristic] Observe players closely, as an anthropologist would, because watching their struggles informs every interface decision. When: When a game introduces a new interface. Why: Watching players act out the interface shows where they struggle and reveals their moment-to-moment intentions.
- [heuristic] When you cannot predict how players will move through a space, leave routes unbuilt at first, watch actual use, and only then build the routes people wear in. When: Designing spaces or paths whose use cannot be predicted in advance. Why: Paving before observing risks locking in routes that do not match real behavior.
- [heuristic] Use developers for repeated, detailed sessions and friends or family for easy, early access. Why: Developers are too close to the game to judge like real players, while friends and family are available and comfortable giving feedback.
- [heuristic] Either result is useful: if players like the game, good; if not, ask why and fix the cause. When: Early in development, while fixes remain possible. Why: Any reaction is useful, and asking a player why they disliked something reveals what to fix.
- [heuristic] Interrupt players only when they do something surprising that you cannot explain. When: Mid-session, when an interruption risks breaking a player's natural play patterns. Why: Asking later may leave the player unable to recall what they were doing, so the moment matters.
- [heuristic] Good playtest questions target specific outcomes like understanding, fairness, boredom, confusion, frustration, dominant strategies, loopholes, hidden bugs, group differences, favorite and least favorite parts, control mapping, level length, puzzle difficulty.
- [heuristic] Some tests should reveal nothing, letting players work out the game on their own. When: When the test aims to see whether players can work out the game unaided. Why: Any hint before play can steer players toward certain things and make them overlook details.
- [heuristic] If parts of the game are irrelevant to the planned questions, consider testing a trimmed version. When: planning a playtest. Why: Irrelevant sections cost session time without answering any planned question.
- [heuristic] A large mismatch between model and real play more often means the model is wrong than that players found an unmodeled exploit. When: When specific cards diverge sharply between model math and community tier lists or market data.
- [heuristic] Small playtests lack data for statistical significance, so focus on key questions and on what answers should look like, then watch for those answers during play. When: running small playtests. Why: Too few testers gives no statistical basis, so pre-set expected answers give something to check.
- [heuristic] End a playtest once the core questions are answered, unless the tester wants to keep playing; ending early respects the tester's time. When: Small-scale playtests of board games or paper prototypes in a small group. Why: Testers give up their time to help, so further play that adds no insight wastes it.
- [heuristic] Run usability observation throughout development and repeat it with each iteration, not only at the end. Why: Testing and redesign are always needed in the development cycle.
- [heuristic] Closed beta and alpha tests can reach larger samples, and even about eight players is better than none. When: When the team can't recruit participants for multi-day lab sessions. Why: These tests explain the reasons behind analytics behavior, which numbers alone cannot show.
- [heuristic] More testers tend to improve a game when individual matches feel like parts of one larger game. Why: Extra players and visual flavor enriched the atmosphere, which made the play feel larger.
- [heuristic] Tailor the observation form to the test focus; interface tests may need fewer overall play-experience questions.
- [heuristic] Keep play sessions to about 15-20 minutes, because testers tend to tire after longer. When: Scheduling playtest play time. Why: Observation needs at least that long, yet testers grow tired beyond it.
- [heuristic] Recruit strangers through many varied channels, since more sources produce a larger and better candidate pool. When: Finding playtesters. Why: Varied channels reach different demographics, so the candidate pool grows broader.
- [heuristic] Playtest results stay trustworthy when testers judge objectively rather than agree with a leader; using playtests to validate a leader's assumptions undermines them. Why: Results come from real players rather than a superior's judgment, so they aren't capped by that person's understanding.
- [heuristic] Focus tests suit both small indie studios and large studios, and small teams without a QA department can use them. When: When a small studio lacks developers or a QA department to test with. Why: Outsiders who receive the game cold reveal how real players actually experience it.
- [heuristic] Testers who know the game but not a specific encounter approximate a player who has played for hours and reached a new challenge. Coworkers can serve. When: testing a specific new encounter. Why: Their knowledge matches a mid-game player.
- [heuristic] Ask a tester to tell the story of what just happened. Elements left out may be dead weight, and the recalled story often differs from the intended one. When: interviewing a tester after a session. Why: Recall reveals what players actually perceived.
- [heuristic] A test protocol is a craft for extracting the most useful knowledge from limited resources, not a set of universally right answers. Why: Flawed protocols give no warning, so their misleading results look plausible and leave designers more confident than the evidence warrants.
- [heuristic] Early playtests address large-scale design questions, while later playtests polish individual moments and geometry pieces. When: As the project iterates and the design matures past its early large-scale questions. Why: scope of questions narrows as design matures.
- [heuristic] If play shows one attribute matters more than its score suggests, raise its weight, for example by doubling its points, then retest.
- [heuristic] Keep veteran players: they are often the most vocal advocates of a game and can point out how to improve it. When: Once long-time players have found most of a game's secrets and its pleasures are starting to wear thin. Why: Long-time players promote the game loudly and know its systems well enough to teach the designer.
- [heuristic] Small playtests are less useful for games with millions of players, so open betas help gather balance data at scale. When: games with very large player bases. Why: Emergent dynamics at huge player counts cannot be seen clearly with a few dozen testers.
- [heuristic] The more asymmetric a game and the harder its sides are to compare mathematically, the more it depends on playtesting before release and analytics after.
- [heuristic] Avoid focus groups unless a very experienced researcher moderates them, because peer pressure introduces many biases. When: Considering group discussion as a research method. Why: Group dynamics distort individual responses.
- [heuristic] Use task analysis for short, specific checks of one task or a single feature. When: When checking whether players can complete one specific task, such as a crafting step or map navigation. Why: Narrow scope lets short sessions produce concrete measures like completion time and error counts.
- [heuristic] If the team does not respond to an idea, reconsider the idea or the approach used to present it.
- [heuristic] Incorporate player feedback on the fly during a paper playtest session. Why: Physical components can be changed at the table, so a player's suggestion can be tried immediately.
- [heuristic] Early design theories are often partly wrong, so test each one in play; even a wrong theory may contain a useful grain of truth.
- [heuristic] If a test seat is unfilled, play it yourself so the rule variant can still be tested. When: no second tester is available.
- [heuristic] Let testers make mistakes, since their confusion reveals rules that need clarifying. When: When running a playtest to see how players handle rules given with no or minimal explanation. Why: Errors expose unclear rules, which flawless play based on explanations would hide.
- [heuristic] Keep self-testing throughout the project, but rely more on outside testers as the game evolves. When: In the foundation stage, while fundamental concepts are still being experimented with.
- [heuristic] Analyzing a memory is easier than analyzing live play; fresh and powerful memories give the best results, though memory is imperfect. When: Analysis is needed after an experience. Why: Memory allows reflection without interrupting the experience.
- [heuristic] Intending to analyze an experience afterward, without letting that intention interfere with the experience, improves recall. When: Playing while planning to review. Why: Expectation of review strengthens memory of the event.
- [heuristic] On paper, circle ideas, sketch, connect them with arrows, and cross things out, then type up the good material later. When: Early brainstorming on paper. Why: Freeform marks, sketches, and arrows capture connections and ideas that text cannot easily express.
- [heuristic] Designers can understand players by using their own modeling, focus, empathy and imagination to notice how those same powers work in players' minds. When: when trying to learn how players think. Why: The designer's own mind shares the player's cognitive tools.
- [heuristic] Examining the interest players actually show is the best measure of an experience's quality. Why: Charting interest over time shows which moments hold players, avoiding the big-picture blindness of judging only the parts.
- [heuristic] Observing which disciplines eat together reveals trust gaps; artists eating apart from programmers may signal pipeline problems, and separate console teams may signal porting problems. Why: Trust forms through frequent face-to-face contact, and people choose dining companions selectively, so meal groupings reveal trust.
- [heuristic] Playing only the first few minutes of a game is usually enough to learn its core mechanics; finishing it is not required. When: analyzing a game's mechanics. Why: Full playthroughs add content the analysis must cover, while the opening minutes already reveal the core mechanics.
- [heuristic] Have other researchers run UX tests when attachment to a feature could bias results. Why: Attachment to the game can bias observation and interpretation.
- [heuristic] Establish a good development process early, built on playtesting, to help a team avoid releasing a game that is dead on arrival. When: From the beginning of the project, before production pressures set in. Why: Without structure, daily crises let objectives vanish and mistakes go unfixed until time runs out.
- [heuristic] Setting a firm play-length target, such as under an hour, can shape a game's whole design. When: early in design for a constrained format.
- [heuristic] Aim to have about 90% of planned content in the game before beta, and leave open slots for later additions. When: preparing for beta testing. Why: Past betas showed that highly skilled players expose balance flaws regardless of how good units seemed, so reserved slots allow late replacements.
- [heuristic] Playtesting during production and QA reveals smaller problems that call for focused changes. When: When production is underway and the broad vision is fixed, so only features can be tinkered with. Why: Early prototyping catches the major flaws first, leaving only minor ones for later testing.
- [heuristic] A game that staff immediately want to replay is likely to be scheduled for publication. Why: An immediate urge to replay signals strong gameplay, and staff who feel it tend to push to get the product published.
- [heuristic] A gameplay video is most credible when rendered from the actual game code. When: making a video for a pitch.
- [heuristic] Watch focus tests in person when possible, since seeing real players is more persuasive than a written summary. Why: Developers who only read summaries may dismiss feedback as unrepresentative, while watching reactions firsthand makes them concrete.
- [heuristic] Talk with testers informally where possible, since a relaxed conversation often reveals more than a feedback form. Why: Testers who don't know the story reveal what they understood or missed, which a relaxed conversation brings out more than a form does.
- [heuristic] Reviewers may understand the project better than you, especially if you are junior, so adjust how much weight you give their comments. Why: Someone who lacks a holistic view of the narrative may judge parts in isolation, while the writer should hold that whole view.
- [heuristic] Testers who already know the story stop noticing minor glitches, so decide how often to bring in new testers. When: planning tester rotation. Why: Repeated exposure to the same plot makes testers overlook small glitches because they already expect what should happen.
- [heuristic] Test ideas under heavy repeated use, since many ideas that sound great once become tiresome by their thousandth use. Why: Only repetition reveals whether a mechanic keeps generating new experiences, since a one-off use has a fixed cost-to-payoff relationship.
- [heuristic] Large beta or post-launch data sets can support analytics-based balancing; use them to update mathematical models so they match real play. When: large beta or post-launch player data is available. Why: Real player data reveals which parts are actually unbalanced, so models built on assumptions can be corrected against observed behaviour.
- [heuristic] When full-scale trials become too costly, build a cheaper apparatus that measures the same properties of the parts. When: Full-size tests are expensive. Why: A bicycle-like rig can push a wing section through air and measure its properties directly, without building full gliders.
- [heuristic] Build spaces and scripts with placeholder geometry during art production rather than waiting for final art, since gameplay can be tested earlier. When: planning production order for levels. Why: Waiting for art delays testing that could begin with placeholder geometry.
- [heuristic] Use screenshots and video to mark moments in play so you can analyze them later. Why: Captured frames provide reference material from which analysis sketches can later be derived.
- [heuristic] Playtesting can reveal recurring layout patterns that either support play or cause unfair deaths, so catalog both kinds deliberately.
- [heuristic] Testers who play to win find optimal solutions and exploits quickly; if one wins using a single option exclusively, that option is probably overpowered.
- [heuristic] Letting players annotate their own video playthroughs reduces memory and cognitive-load bias in post-experience annotation. Why: Reviewing the recording lets players annotate what they actually saw, rather than reconstructing the experience from memory afterward.
- [heuristic] Unanimous agreement in a team often signals mental laziness or fear rather than real cohesion. When: when a design team shows no disagreement in discussions. Why: Agreement without thought hides flaws, and fear discourages people from voicing dissent.
- [heuristic] Balance can be protected by structure: similar starts so late entry is not a disadvantage, late-game trades that are hard to exploit, and a game length that ends before inflation becomes severe. Why: Resource output climbs steadily over the game, yet the ending arrives before that growth breaks the economy.
- [heuristic] If power can be measured, tune game length by trial and error, adjusting values and replaying until length lands in the target range. When: power is measurable. Why: Measured power over time shows how each numeric change shifts the ending point, so adjustments can be checked directly.
- [heuristic] Use at least one tester per supported play style who always plays the same way, to confirm each intended strategy is actually viable. When: When the game supports several distinct play styles. Why: Such testers may be the only ones trying a given strategy.
- [heuristic] Make large changes when the right value is unknown, such as doubling or halving; when more confident, change by at least 10 percent, since smaller changes often go unnoticed. When: Adjusting numeric values during tuning. Why: Small changes can be invisible in play.
- [heuristic] A simulation should roughly match an exact calculation; if it does not, check both the math and the simulation. When: both exact and simulated answers are available. Why: Agreement between independent methods raises confidence in both, while a mismatch could originate in either one.
- [heuristic] Before fixing an imbalance, ask why it happens and what causes it, then fix it in the system where the root cause lives rather than in an interconnected system. When: Diagnosing balance problems reported in playtesting. Why: Imbalances can spread to other systems, so the reported symptom is not always the thing to fix.
- [heuristic] Expert players play more and at a higher skill level than designers, so changes after beta are likely to be needed. When: planning post-beta balance work. Why: Such players log far more hours than the design team and at higher skill, so they expose weaknesses the designers never reached.
- [procedure] 1) Solo play to remove obvious problems that waste others' time. 2) Close friends, co-workers or other designers for outside opinions. 3) Friends-of-friends or strangers while observing or collecting one-on-one feedback. When: Across development, moving from early solo play to later tests with strangers. Why: Each audience widens in familiarity and reveals different problems. (x2)
- [procedure] Ask six post-play questions in this order: 1) Ask what was most frustrating. 2) Ask the favorite moment. 3) Ask what they wanted to do but could not. 4) Ask what they would change, add, or remove. 5) Ask what they were trying to do. 6) Ask how they would describe the game to friends. When: after a playtest session. Why: The first question is placed early so players can voice frustration before the rest of the questions.
- [procedure] 1) State the specific questions the playtest must answer. 2) Pick testers who match those questions. 3) Choose the development stage for the test. 4) Pick a venue suited to the questions. 5) List what to watch for, including unexpected things. 6) Plan how to capture information during and after play. When: before running a playtest.
- [procedure] 1) Pre-idea: gather a focus group of target players and ask what they enjoy. 2) Concept: describe the idea and ask for reactions. 3) Paper prototype: have players try a barely playable version. 4) Whitebox: test without art, stating caveats first. 5) Working prototype: keep testing as late as possible. 6) Shipped: treat post-launch feedback as ongoing playtesting. When: Across the full development cycle, from the earliest idea through launch. Why: Each stage gives different information, and testing continues across all of them.
- [procedure] 1) Decide the question first, such as whether the game is fun or balanced. 2) Design the playtest so it answers that question. When: planning any playtest. Why: Because the play serves a research purpose, the session must be built around the question it is meant to answer.
- [procedure] To check whether one item is overpowered in a specific situation, force that situation, such as arranging the deck so the item is drawn early. When: When asking whether one item is overpowered in a particular game situation. Why: The situation must actually occur during the test, or the balance question goes unanswered.
- [procedure] 1) Log each relevant variable, such as button state, physics state or a flag, every frame. 2) Draw each one as a line on a scrolling graph. 3) Mark events such as leap, touchdown, drop, high leap, delayed leap and crouch as labelled vertical lines. 4) While paused, scroll and zoom to the area where the problem occurred. When: when players report that controls feel wrong. Why: Frame-by-frame recording preserves exact input and physics timing, so a control problem can be examined after it happens.
- [procedure] 1) Introduce yourself, thank testers, state the purpose, and disclose any recording or observers (2-3 min). 2) Warm-up questions about games they play (about 5 min). 3) Play session (15-20 min). 4) Discussion probing appeal, interest, challenge, and understanding (15-20 min). 5) Wrap up: thank testers, keep contact details, optionally give a small gift. When: Running a playtest session with outside testers. Why: Timed parts keep the designer focused on observing and stop sessions running past the point where testers tire.
- [procedure] 1) Collect enough applicants first. 2) Screen for people who explain their opinions clearly, for example in a short phone call. 3) Ask about hobbies, motive for responding, and how often they buy this type of game. When: Selecting playtesters from applicants. Why: Articulate testers give usable feedback, and genre habits predict how they judge your game.
- [procedure] 1) Obtain approval from the company's leadership and production staff to show the build outside the studio. 2) Have every tester sign a non-disclosure agreement. 3) Recruit outside players from the target audience, run the session yourself or hire a focus-testing agency, and give them the game with no instructions. When: before inviting outside testers. Why: Outside players give realistic feedback on an unexplained game.
- [procedure] 1) Watch testers' reactions and struggles without explaining the game. 2) Afterward, ask how they experienced particular elements. When: during and after a focus test. Why: Testers with no briefing meet the game as a real player would, so their struggles reflect real first contact.
- [procedure] 1) Early in development, have participants test a prototype with specific tasks, such as finding and crafting an item. 2) Later, have them play as they would at home. When: Early on, using a prototype or early version; later, once the game is more advanced.
- [procedure] 1) Record mistakes, long confusion, and missed important information. 2) Follow observation with a survey using non-leading questions, such as asking what an icon represents. When: After an observation phase with playtest participants. Why: Leading wording steers respondents, so survey questions must stay objective and not influence their answers.
- [procedure] 1) Watch where players look, what they do, and their faces. 2) Ask players to tell the story of what happened. 3) Test knowledge of noticed details. 4) Ask players to walk through a decision. When: when watching is not enough. Why: Behaviour shows some of what players perceived, but their internal understanding can only be reached by asking.
- [procedure] 1) When a tester suggests a change, identify the experience behind it. 2) Test whether the cause is aiming, ammo economy, or a misunderstood feature. 3) Fix the underlying problem. When: When a tester starts making suggestions for changes to the game. Why: Suggestions at face value hide the real problem.
- [procedure] 1) Watch an unfamiliar tester play without speaking. 2) Note each failure. 3) Apply a fix, often ready within half a day. 4) Repeat with a fresh tester, about 10 to 20 cycles over two or three weeks. When: after self-testing no longer reveals new problems. Why: Fresh testers expose failures the designer cannot see.
- [procedure] 1) Ask why the player chose a path. 2) If they mention an element and why they rejected it, they perceived it. 3) If not, they may never have noticed it. When: When checking whether a player noticed a specific element of the game. Why: Asking directly about an element makes players answer yes to seem knowledgeable, which corrupts the result.
- [procedure] 1) Place a short text box of two to three lines below the game. 2) Ask players how to make the game better. 3) Allow one message per session, then hide the box. 4) Route messages to a mailing list all team members can join. When: When collecting player feedback during an alpha or live test. Why: Despite expected abuse, players sent mostly high-quality suggestions through the box.
- [procedure] 1) Prepare a private space with a camera and the questions visible. 2) Let each participant enter alone, read one question, reflect, and answer on camera. 3) Share the recordings with the team. When: When collecting interview or focus group responses. Why: Individual answers counter groupthink and let quieter participants contribute.
- [procedure] 1) Set up pop-up targets at varied distances. 2) Observe whether players overshoot or stop short. 3) Make controls less sensitive if they overshoot, and less stiff if they stop short. When: Tuning twitch-skill controls. Why: Overshooting signals excess sensitivity and stopping short signals excess stiffness.
- [procedure] Compile moderators' notes at the end of the day and compare them across observers. When: Synthesizing observations from a UX test. Why: Cross-checking notes reduces individual observer error.
- [procedure] Recruit participants matching the target audience profile; allow at least a week for recruiting a good sample. When: Planning UX test recruitment. Why: Matching the audience and allowing time improves validity.
- [procedure] 1) Record your own actions and feelings while playing a game. 2) Observe another player of the same game and note their behavior. 3) Compare the two sets of notes.
- [procedure] 1) Play and note the choices you make. 2) Record how you felt. 3) Identify the underlying mechanics behind the experience. When: each day of play. Why: Regular written reflection on choices and mechanics builds a personal way of thinking about games, which designers need.
- [procedure] 1) Write more questions than you need. 2) Rank them by importance. 3) Group the top questions by category. When: When drafting a feedback questionnaire for a playtest.
- [procedure] 1) Test the prototype for completeness. 2) Watch for impasses, rule questions, and judgment calls. 3) If players argue or reach a dead end, revise and test again. When: Testing a prototype for completeness. Why: Rules that seem clear to the designer hide gaps that only show up when players actually try to apply them.
- [procedure] 1) Use control situations to isolate parts of the system. 2) Run playtests where familiar testers try to disrupt the system. 3) Recruit players who enjoy subversive solutions and challenge them to find the most creative subversion. Why: Released games are played by far more people than any testing team can recruit, and one player may find a flaw others missed.
- [procedure] Test checkpoint spacing: 1) place only one or two checkpoints in a level 2) playtest and note how failing feels 3) add checkpoints at the start of each challenge scene 4) retest and ask which retries felt better. When: When a level builds from a safe tutorial to challenging puzzles that players may fail and retry.
- [procedure] Test whether cues teach better: 1) graybox a level teaching two or three mechanics, including movement 2) add sound and graphics that trigger in teaching areas 3) test graphics only, sound only, then both 4) log with several playtesters which works best. When: When a level teaches two or three mechanics, including basic movement.
- [procedure] 1) Have a player who has never played the game play it. 2) Observe whether they get stuck often or follow potential pathways clearly. 3) If they get stuck, identify what the game could do to communicate pathways better. When: When testing whether a game's exploration pathways are understandable to new players. Why: New players reveal communication gaps that designers miss.
- [procedure] 1) Mentally play the unbuilt game as a player would, emulating its systems. 2) Track what happens, what is known and unknown, which decisions appear, and the thinking each needs. 3) Move through every second in real time, including animations, loading screens and idle button presses. When: analyzing decision pacing before a prototype exists. Why: The mind naturally skips to the most interesting parts of imagined or remembered experiences, hiding the flow gaps that need finding.
- [procedure] 1) Have the experience without stopping. 2) Replay it in memory while analyzing everything, taking notes if useful. When: Analyzing a game or other experience. Why: The first pass preserves the experience; the second pass allows reflection on how it felt and why.
- [procedure] 1) Take brief, quick looks at the experience, judging whether it is engaging enough. 2) Return immediately to the experience. 3) Repeat habitually so the looks go unnoticed. When: Observing play experience without disrupting it. Why: Brief glances interrupt less than sustained analysis and are less noticeable to oneself.
- [procedure] 1) Roll up and store posted sheets. 2) Unroll them later. 3) Resume the session as if it had never stopped, even weeks or a year later. When: Brainstorming that spans long gaps. Why: Stored sheets preserve the layout of earlier ideas.
- [procedure] 1) Ask the client what problem they are trying to solve. 2) Confirm the underlying problem with the team. 3) Find a fix that solves that problem, even if it differs from the client's suggestion. When: When a client proposes an idea that seems wrong. Why: Client suggestions that seem wrong usually answer an unstated problem, so finding that problem allows a better fix.
- [procedure] 1) Right-click a floor in the editor and choose Play from here. 2) Press Esc to return to the editor. 3) To test the whole level from the start, use Play this level in an editor window.
- [procedure] 1) Bring the writer, who knows the dialogue's intent best. 2) Bring a character biography sheet with background and reference voices. 3) Bring a pronunciation sheet of phonetic spellings, so every actor says each word the same way. When: Preparing for a voice recording session. Why: Shared intent and pronunciation keep delivery consistent across actors and sessions.
- [procedure] 1) Record narrative lines in linear story order so actors track their character's arc. 2) Group lines by mood to avoid switching emotions. 3) Schedule shouting or strenuous lines near the end of the session. When: Planning the order of a dialogue recording session. Why: Story order and mood grouping ease performance, and strained voices should not affect other lines.
- [procedure] 1) Split a character's kit into individual moves. 2) For each move, measure frequency per match, hit rate and total damage per match. 3) Compute total damage as frequency × hit rate × damage. 4) Rank moves by share of total damage. When: Evaluating whether a fighter's strength or frustration comes from a few specific moves. Why: Overall win rate hides which moves drive damage and how often they are used.
- [procedure] 1) Segment move usage and win contribution by skill level. 2) Identify which moves dominate low-rated matches. 3) Compare those moves against high-rated usage before changing values. When: Finding which specific moves drive a skill-dependent imbalance. Why: Moves that dominate one skill band may be irrelevant to another.
- [procedure] 1) Compare final standings with rating-predicted rankings. 2) Measure how often players finish differently than expected. When: Validating a rating system in free-for-all games. Why: Multi-player finishes test predictions beyond win or loss.
- [procedure] Run regular UX tests on the work, including tests of iconography, to check how players actually read inputs. Why: Perception is subjective and biased by prior knowledge, context and expectations, so players may not read inputs as designers intend.
- [procedure] 1) Meet the main stakeholders after the report. 2) Review each issue and classify it as intentional, to be redone soon, or to be fixed and re-tested later. 3) Track fixes in a shared project-tracking tool so progress is visible. When: after a user research report. Why: Stakeholders reveal which issues are by design or about to be redone, and visible tracking gives the team a sense of progress.
- [procedure] 1) Run the UX test. 2) Identify changes everyone agrees on. 3) Assign those changes as tasks. When: After a UX test.
- [procedure] 1) Warn participants before the test. 2) Monitor their discomfort during play. 3) If aftereffects occur, allow at least 45 minutes of readjustment, especially before driving. When: When conducting UX tests of a VR game. Why: Development builds often have performance issues, mainly input-to-feedback latency, which raise cybersickness risk.
- [procedure] 1) Invite dissidents to speak. 2) Clarify vague statements. 3) Point out healthy consensus. 4) Draw parallels between different ideas. 5) Ask people to explain ideas clearly and constructively, then combine and build on them. When: When moderating an ideation focus group. Why: These techniques keep the discussion from lingering on a single topic and push it forward.
- [procedure] 1) Start a test session already in the rare state. 2) Vary the relevant conditions. 3) Observe results over a short period. When: testing rare game events. Why: Rare events may never occur by chance during a session, so forcing them lets testers repeat them under varied conditions.
- [procedure] 1) Ask testers to plot the game on the play matrix. 2) Ask whether outcomes depend more on chance or skill, and on mental or physical ability. 3) Ask which direction they would move it and why. When: gathering tester perceptions of game nature. Why: Audiences tend to cluster in particular quadrants, so testers' preferences reveal where the game belongs.
- [procedure] 1) In the foundation stage, test basic functionality without judging balance or fairness. 2) In later stages, hunt loopholes and dead ends, and check interface accessibility and navigation. When: planning test focus across development.
- [procedure] 1) Seat each participant at a partitioned station with headphones. 2) Collect opinions through a web questionnaire. 3) Keep participants isolated so one participant's opinions do not influence another's. When: Running group playtests where independent opinions matter. Why: Isolation prevents participants from influencing each other.
- [procedure] 1) Pick one type of choice. 2) Add an element of surprise to its outcome. 3) Observe how the gameplay changes. When: Testing the effect of surprise on a choice. Why: Surprise adds drama and tension, but if every choice were random, players would feel their choices meant nothing.
- [procedure] 1) Ask a math-minded person nearby. 2) If none is available, post the question on a forum or mailing list. 3) Describe the question as probably too hard, or frame it as a puzzle. 4) Do not hold back questions that seem naive. When: a probability problem beyond basic rules and simulation, too hard to solve alone. Why: Experts often enjoy solving problems that others consider too difficult.
- [procedure] 1) Group mechanics into logical categories. 2) Give each category its own subtotal column. 3) Feed the subtotal columns into the final balance column. When: a balance formula combines many traits or mechanics. Why: Each part becomes easier to understand and check.
- [procedure] 1) Add an in-game button players press to flag a perceived problem. 2) Record it alongside game data. 3) Review recordings at flagged moments. When: Perceived problems that are rare or hard to reproduce in playtests. Why: Flagged moments show exactly what players experienced.
- [procedure] 1) Watch playtesters struggle with the content. 2) Question them afterward about what they believed it was. 3) Compare their answers with the intended premise to find missing context. When: When playtesters struggle with content that requires prerequisite knowledge they may lack. Why: Testers lacking background knowledge may misread the content, which questioning them afterward reveals.
- [procedure] 1) Play your own game as each player type would. 2) Note where that type's goals are blocked or served. 3) Adjust the design to fit each type. When: testing a design from several player perspectives. Why: Designers can test how a design serves different player types by playing as each.
- [procedure] 1) Pick one unknown ability and evaluate it alone. 2) Change one variable at a time to measure its effect. 3) Finish it before examining the next, because two unknowns analyzed together give no clear individual costs. When: more than one new ability is unknown. Why: combined unknowns hide each ability's individual cost.
- [procedure] 1) Put real playtest time or turns on the horizontal axis. 2) Put a numeric power formula for each player on the vertical axis. 3) Plot each player's power to see how the game unfolds. When: measuring a playtested game's length or power balance. Why: Balancing requires power curves that make the game end within a target time range, and the plot shows when key points are reached.
- [procedure] 1) Fill a row with random die results, one column per die. 2) Sum each row. 3) Fill the rows down for thousands of trials. 4) Divide the rows meeting the target by the total rows. When: code is not available. Why: Listing every combination of eight dice is impractically large, so random trials estimate the share instead.
- [metric] Simple constrained games may need two or three playtests, shooter combat around six to twelve, and open systems-driven games many more. When: Estimating how many playtests a game kind needs before its design is understood. Why: Game types differ in how much experience they generate, so the required testing varies.
- [metric] Confidence in an untested element can rise from about 40 percent on paper to about 90 percent after several playtests. When: When a base system has gone through several rounds of revision and trial play. Why: Elements built on a settled foundation are not disrupted by later changes cascading upward from below.
- [metric] A total score on the observation grid summarizes the intensity of the flow state across its three dimensions. When: when using the observation grid.
- [metric] Task analysis tests should use at least 15 participants. When: when planning a task analysis test. Why: smaller samples weaken the evidence.
- [metric] Plan about 50 to 80 lines per hour for ensembles with typical 10-word lines, and about 100 lines per hour for a solo actor averaging two takes per line. When: Estimating studio time for dialogue sessions. Why: Realistic rates prevent under-scheduling sessions.
- [metric] Chance a positive result is real equals prevalence times sensitivity, divided by that product plus (1 - prevalence) times (1 - specificity). When: When testing for a condition that is rare in the population. Why: true positives divided by all positive results.
- [metric] Large samples reveal small differences that playtests cannot measure, such as a character winning 55% of matches. When: Balancing with metrics from many players. Why: Small effects need large samples to be distinguished from noise.
- [tradeoff] Interrupting players with questions reveals insight but disturbs natural play, and constant questioning can ruin the session; waiting until the end risks players forgetting the moment. When: When a player does something surprising that the designer cannot explain. Why: Interrupting can disrupt natural play, while waiting until the end means the player may no longer recall the moment.
- [tradeoff] A studio is convenient and lets the whole team watch real players, but unfamiliar surroundings can make testers uneasy. Why: Testers in an unfamiliar workplace with colleagues watching may hold back noise, fun, and candid opinions.
