- [tradeoff] Public venues such as malls, campus events, games festivals, or street tables are cheap and reach many testers, but finding the right demographic and avoiding distraction are harder. Why: Competing activities at a public venue pull testers' attention away from the session.
- [tradeoff] Testing at the playtester's home shows natural play and social interaction, but few observers fit and hardware may need carrying. Why: Home play mirrors how buyers will actually use the game, including social settings.
- [tradeoff] Online testing reaches many machine configurations and suits stress and massively multiplayer questions, but gives less insight and makes secrecy harder. When: When the questions concern stress testing or massively multiplayer play. Why: Many testers on varied hardware play at once, but without observers the insight into their behavior is thinner.
- [tradeoff] Multi-day lab sessions give deep data but are hard to recruit for, while remote tools are easier to run but make observation harder.
- [tradeoff] Group testing generates ideas well but evaluates them poorly, while one-on-one testing is better suited to evaluating ideas.
- [tradeoff] Strangers give objective, fresh perspectives that close contacts cannot, so use strangers when objectivity matters most. When: Choosing between friends and strangers as testers. Why: Close contacts bring familiarity and bias that blur judgment.
- [tradeoff] Developers can watch from a one-way mirror, which keeps them out of the room, or together in a room, which sparks useful discussion. When: Arranging observer seating for a test. Why: Separate viewing reduces interference, while shared viewing aids discussion.
- [tradeoff] Use eyetracking or biometrics only when budget justifies them and for short, specific moments; efficient observation is usually better otherwise. When: Choosing lab instruments. Why: Expensive instruments cost more than their insight in most tests.
- [tradeoff] Observation suits small groups and early prototypes; analytics needs large samples, so near launch beta-scale play data guides balance once observers are impractical to recruit. When: Choosing a balance method based on player scale. Why: Large player counts make observation-based playtesting impractical.
- [tradeoff] Programmers supplying navigation data directly means every new level needs new data, which hurts reuse across games and makes simple level changes difficult. When: When the game level changes. Why: The AI's knowledge of the environment comes from programmer-supplied data tied to each layout, so it cannot be carried over.
- [tradeoff] Ensemble recording makes dialogue flow naturally and builds chemistry, but adds pressure, ego conflicts, misunderstandings and retakes; solo sessions are easier to plan. When: Choosing how to record a dialogue scene. Why: Actors reacting to each other sound more natural but cost more time and coordination.
- [tradeoff] Lab research reveals causes in depth but struggles to predict how much a lab-observed behavior matters across a larger audience. When: when deciding whether a lab finding needs scale validation. Why: small samples cannot estimate how common a behavior is.
- [tradeoff] An imagined real-time walkthrough is less accurate than playtesting but far cheaper and easier, and it is better than no pacing analysis. When: before playtesting is possible. Why: The imagined run tends to skip ahead to exciting moments and hide flow gaps, so every second must be walked through in order.
- [pitfall] Testers close to the game, such as friends, family or invited guests, give politer feedback, may bend the truth and like it, so they overlook flaws and hide problems. Why: Friends and family avoid hurting feelings and already like the designer, so they predispose toward liking the game. (x2)
- [pitfall] Developers and designers are biased and a poor proxy for target players: insiders bring skills the audience lacks and may misjudge difficulty, such as making a children's game too hard. When: When the game targets a specific audience, such as children. Why: Insiders have skills the target audience lacks, so their judgment of difficulty is unreliable. (x2)
- [pitfall] Relying only on playtesting has limits: early decisions precede any playable game, playtesters may reject unfinished unusual ideas, and playtests are occasional while decisions are daily. When: early design decisions. Why: Decisions arrive daily but playtests happen only now and then, so the designer's own judgment fills the gap.
- [pitfall] A vague goal like 'is my game fun?' cannot guide a session and wastes playtesting time. Why: A playtest is a prototype of the experience, so it needs a specific question to answer, as any prototype does.
- [pitfall] Focus groups suit ranking well-defined features, but are often poorly run or manipulated. When: When ranking the importance of clearly specified features.
- [pitfall] A careless remark before play starts can spoil the whole test. When: When briefing players before a playtest that needs some introduction. Why: Players' behavior is shaped by the briefing, so later observations may reflect the words rather than the game.
- [pitfall] A sample can be large yet unrepresentative of the target audience, skewing conclusions; a playtest that looks large can still mislead if its composition differs from the market. When: When recruiting testers for playtest data collection. Why: Sample composition determines whether feedback reflects the target audience.
- [pitfall] Testing broken or incomplete features wastes time; features must work, though placeholder visuals are acceptable.
- [pitfall] Common excuses for skipping playtests are tight schedules, fear of changing beloved ideas, cost, and the belief that testing is marketing's job. Why: Feedback arriving at the final stage of production leaves no room to alter core elements.
- [pitfall] Testers who share a room and coordinate informally can mask designs that depend on team coordination, which strangers playing online will expose. When: testing multiplayer designs. Why: Strangers lack shared context and tactics.
- [pitfall] Focus testing is often used as a synonym for playtesting, which is incorrect because it answers a different question. Why: It is a market-research method where groups discuss ideas, and it does not need a playable build.
- [pitfall] Recruiting minors requires extra legal care. When: Recruiting participants who may be under age. Why: Minors carry additional legal protections.
- [pitfall] Spending weeks on an engine or infrastructure before testing the core idea means the prototype has failed. When: When a project has run for weeks or months with an engine but no test of the core idea. Why: Engine work has nothing to do with proving the core design idea, which is what a prototype is for.
- [pitfall] One negative tester can skew results, so do not draw conclusions from a single person's feedback. Why: A single negative participant can color the whole group's reactions.
- [pitfall] A small group of confidants cannot provide the broad, objective criticism needed in later stages. When: Once the game is playable with clearly defined rules and is maturing. Why: Personal relationships blur objectivity, so confidants tend to be either too harsh or too forgiving.
- [pitfall] Trusting players' survey recollections at face value ignores that memory is reconstructed at recall time, so reports are biased rather than exact. When: When players answer post-play surveys. Why: Answers reflect a remembered, often biased, account of the experience rather than the experience as it happened.
- [pitfall] Misclassification can take extensive playtesting to find and fix, and weighted nodes make it worse.
- [pitfall] Surface listening takes people's words at face value and misses what they actually mean. When: Gathering feedback from others. Why: Words alone can mislead.
- [pitfall] Rationalizing away testing, such as believing a design is so good it needs no testing, leads to repeated costly failures. Why: Games are too complex to predict build and bug-fix time, and must also be fun, so only repeated testing exposes problems.
- [pitfall] Strike probabilities computed from steady-state values misrepresent early turns, which must be analyzed separately because the starting state differs. When: When examining early rolls before steady state, such as first-player advantage in a two-player game. Why: the answer depends on steady-state probabilities, not the starting state.
- [pitfall] Presuming every player interprets an identical stimulus identically leads to misread interfaces. Why: Perception is subjective and shaped by prior knowledge, context, and expectations, so one stimulus can be read differently.
- [pitfall] Unremoved biases in testing lead to wrong conclusions.
- [pitfall] Vocal players, such as forum posters, may not represent the majority, so their feedback should not be treated as representative. When: When relying on forum posts or complaints that reach customer service. Why: A few players repeatedly voice the same complaints, so volume does not equal majority opinion.
- [pitfall] Group pressure can make apparent consensus look stronger than it is, and dissent is suppressed because disagreeing takes effort and social courage. When: When reading focus group results. Why: Disagreeing takes social courage and mental effort, so people hold back differences from the group.
- [pitfall] Beta testing cannot reproduce the scale and variety of release play.
- [pitfall] In group tests participants may hide confusion or copy a neighbor, so test one-on-one unless the game requires multiplayer. When: running accessibility tests with several testers. Why: Social pressure and copying distort what the test reveals.
- [pitfall] Feedback that does not move the project forward wastes everyone's time. When: When critiquing concept art or sketches produced by artists. Why: Even an imperfect sketch can hold useful elements, so criticism should look for them before speaking.
- [pitfall] Stopping after one test, rather than repeating the loop, leaves design problems unresolved. Why: The experience resides in people's perception rather than the design, so findings must feed redesign and retesting.
- [pitfall] Assuming moderation tools have solved antisocial behavior; it remains far from solved in online games.
- [pitfall] Hidden input complexity is costly to build and hard to review, so teams may underinvest in it.
- [pitfall] A convincing result in one's own play or one designer's reaction does not guarantee that other players will respond the same way. When: When validating a feature only through personal testing. Why: Players differ in how they perceive threat, safety, and fun.
- [pitfall] Expecting psychology to answer all design questions about experience is a mistake, because it is useful but does not cover experience directly. Why: Its scientific standards exclude much of subjective experience.
- [pitfall] Breaking a motif's link before it has been established through repetition weakens the surprise it was meant to create. When: When a musical warning has been repeatedly paired with a character before a twist. Why: Surprise depends on violating an expectation the audience was conditioned to form through repeated pairing.
- [pitfall] Repeating one simple action with long waits can feel manipulative to players. When: In mobile free-to-play games requiring long waits between repeated simple actions. Why: The repeated single action mirrors a reinforcement-conditioning model, which is why the term is used derogatorily.
- [pitfall] Treating one streak as proof that the random number generator is broken is a false alarm when the player base is large enough for rare streaks to occur regularly. When: large player base or many attempts. Why: Within a large pool of random rolls, short unlikely runs almost always show up somewhere, so one streak says nothing about the generator.
- [pitfall] A simulation gives an approximate result that changes from run to run, so it is a ballpark estimate rather than an exact answer. When: When a ballpark answer is enough and an exact result is not required. Why: Repeated random trials only approximate the true value, so each run lands close to the answer but not exactly on it.
- [pitfall] Testing an enemy alone in isolation, rather than alongside other enemies, can miss a flaw that appears only in combination. When: Testing encounters before a whole-game playtest. Why: Isolation removes the interactions that expose exploits.
- [pitfall] Assuming average LTV is the same across games is a mistake, since it varies widely. Why: Most free-to-play players spend nothing while a handful spend heavily, so the average swings a lot between games.
- [fact] Watching people play reveals problems the team never anticipated; these unplanned surprises are among the most valuable findings because they show what the designer does not yet understand. (x2)
- [fact] Playtesting is arguably the most important filter; getting to a playable build early reveals needed changes and tunes the other filters.
- [fact] Friends and family are highly available and comfortable giving feedback, and often share ideas after the session.
- [fact] Playtesting finds problems early, builds confidence that the game suits its audience, and is essential to making a good game.
- [fact] Asking what a player was doing reveals their goal, and asking how they would describe the game reveals the point they perceive.
- [fact] A playtesting lab offers one-way mirrors, cameras, and expert note-takers, but is usually expensive; some third parties offer lab testing.
- [fact] Watching a playtest in person reveals more than surveys or recordings alone.
- [fact] Cognitive load is hard to measure, since brain scanning during playtests is impractical and restricted in some countries.
- [fact] Playthrough tests explain why players do what telemetry records, adding context that analytics alone cannot give.
- [fact] Watching other people use a prototype improves the designer's judgment.
- [fact] Four playtest structures are one-on-one observation, group play, feedback forms, and oral interviews.
- [fact] Group play suits physical prototypes and digital prototypes run in a lab with several computers.
- [fact] Playtesting is essential to design work, cannot be hurried or skipped, and keeps the designer seeing the game as players will first encounter it. Why: Playtesters act as the designer's eyes and ears, showing how the game feels in practice.
- [fact] Playtesting ranges from informal sessions with 10 to 20 people to large facilities; one large studio project logged over 3000 hours with more than 600 players.
- [fact] A functional game has components that interact properly and a reachable resolution; players in a paper prototype follow rules without reaching an impasse.
- [fact] Judging functionality is a matter of judgment, since testing every permutation under all conditions is impractical.
- [fact] A small representative sample of about eight participants is usually enough in a playtest to reveal mistakes and delays through observation.
- [fact] Watching playtests reveals difficulty that is too hard, instant victory that signals too easy, and missed instructions or opportunities that signal unclear design.
- [fact] Self-testing is the cheapest playtest protocol and reveals flow, pacing, balance and technical bugs. Early iteration loops should end with self-tests.
- [fact] Playtests aim to reveal player confusion, difficulty imbalance, overall balance, degenerate strategies, and whether the story is understood.
- [fact] Game testing includes playtesting, usability testing, QA testing and focus testing, and each returns a different kind of knowledge.
- [fact] Playtesting ranks as the top form of testing for games.
- [fact] An in-game suggestion box can yield around thirty messages per day with high-quality content, including bug reports, usability problems, and good ideas. When: When a sample of players is exposed to the feedback box.
- [fact] Free tools such as OBS record gameplay and webcam feed, and input overlays can show controller or keyboard input and local time to measure task duration. When: Setting up recording for a UX test. Why: Recordings and overlays provide evidence for reports and timing.
- [fact] Strong opposition to card decisions led fans to build their own playtest variants, each involving roughly 4000 cards.
- [fact] Target-audience testers can compare your game with others they have played, providing market research.
- [fact] Companies usually pay testers in cash or free games, while independent and personal projects typically pay nothing and testers gain satisfaction instead.
- [fact] The risks of using testers are small compared with other production risks.
- [fact] Early playtests usually reveal many problems, so the designer must decide which to tackle first.
- [fact] Lessons from one playtest do not carry over reliably to the next game; detailing is learned through practice.
- [fact] Digital games hide many loopholes because their possibilities are too numerous for designers to test exhaustively.
- [fact] Objectivity is the team communication key most likely to go wrong.
- [fact] A player walkthrough usually appears too late to change the game it covers, but its findings help the next project. When: After release.
- [fact] Population size does not change the chance that a positive result is correct, but a larger population flags more healthy people wrongly.
- [fact] Probability can state the chance of each outcome but cannot predict any single actual result.
- [fact] UX analysis gives a less biased view of how consumers react than creative intuition alone.
- [fact] A later revision of UX maturity uses six levels, listed from highest to lowest: User-Driven, Integrated, Structured, Emergent, Limited, and Absent.
- [fact] Design, art, engineering, marketing, business intelligence, and executives all contribute to the player experience.
- [fact] A geopolitical review may be done by a localization team member or by external services for large games.
- [fact] A geopolitical review covers the whole game: menus, writing, graphics, packaging, audio and branding.
- [fact] Testers want the best and most fun experience for players, so even tough feedback serves the same goal as yours.
- [fact] Common responses to antisocial play are a code of conduct, player reporting, and temporary bans for confirmed violators.
- [fact] Field testing can reveal behavior that calculations miss; the placement of a single part can change how a craft fails.
- [fact] Playtests can show whether environmental teaching methods are sufficient and whether players appreciate the narrative tone.
- [fact] Six tester types reveal different problems: the winner, the angry tester, the same-approach tester, the newcomer, the griefer, and the pro-gamer who dislikes luck.
- [fact] Most extreme experiments fail, so their knobs usually must be turned back down.
- [fact] Repeated simulation captures real-world factors, such as a weighted die, that the theoretical math leaves out.
- [fact] For an executive, the key issue is the commercial revenue a game can generate and its scale. When: Pitching to an executive. Why: Executives judge a pitch mainly by commercial return.
- [fact] Deep specialized knowledge that rivals struggle to match is a barrier.
- [fact] Tabletop designers can rely on software simulations to tune their titles, so the same randomness standards apply to them.
- [fact] Linking UX issues to retention or revenue drops is hard because many factors affect those metrics at once.

## iteration
- [model] Design has four steps: foundations, where the core idea is tested for fun; structure, where enough rules exist for people without your vision to play; formal details; refinement.
- [model] Playtesting follows a continuous loop of playtest, evaluate and revise, repeated from the first moment of design onward. Why: Testing late leaves no room for fundamental change, and a weak core mechanic cannot be fixed at that point.
- [model] The formal details stage has three goals: make the game functional, make it internally complete, and make it balanced. When: Once a promising core idea needs a fully working version built out.
- [model] Design thinking manages creativity through repeated cycles of generating ideas, prototyping, testing, and iterating until the design satisfies from a UX standpoint.
- [model] Design thinking runs in three stages: understand users, explore through ideas and prototypes, and materialize through testing and iteration.
- [model] Three levels: vision (why: concepts, purpose), organization (how: systems, links, org and flow charts), execution (what: breakdowns, asset lists, technical, legal and human constraints). Why: The top level sets goals, the middle defines the means to reach them, and the bottom accepts real-world limits so the work can actually be produced.
- [model] Development has five stages: concept, preproduction, production, QA/polish, and maintenance.
- [model] A complete game design covers four tetrad elements: technology, mechanics, story, and aesthetics.
- [model] Iteration is a loop of short-range planning, implementation, and testing, where each test checks assumptions against reality and informs the next cycle. Why: Planning need only extend to the end of the current loop, since each test supplies reliable knowledge for planning the next one.
- [model] Real knowledge creation is organic: many activities overlap, testing is unscheduled, and the approach adapts daily, so no fixed loop fully describes it. Why: Invention is an ever-changing frontier rather than an assembly line.
- [model] Decisions have eight kinds of effects: design, implementation cost, immaturity burden, critical failure risk, process burden, political, cultural, and decision cost. When: When evaluating a design decision beyond its effect on the game. Why: Good decisions require considering effects on processes, people, businesses and markets, not just the game itself.
- [model] Play can be structured in three levels: newbies, who get a separate, low-stakes challenge; players, who face the main challenge; and elders, who create challenges for others. When: When designing a game community. Why: Newbies are challenged by learning the game itself, so that learning process must be rewarding or they will quit before engaging.
- [model] Each card has three stats: Cost is paid in health at reveal, Strength decides battles, and Damage is dealt if the card stays in play. When: Simultaneous-reveal card duels.
- [model] A designer must listen to five targets: team, audience, game, client, and self. The five are interconnected. Why: Design decisions are linked to each other, so listening to one group feeds into what the others need.
- [model] Five technology bets most likely to lead toward a better world: magical interfaces, fair payment, less artificial AI, play with family and friends, and self-transformation. Why: Players who lose interest in new games have lost faith that designers know the way.
- [model] Skill mastery in games passes through three reinventions: the manual level of mechanical interface skill, the situational level of reading and deciding, and the mental level of concentration and psychological play. When: As players progress up a game's skill range.
- [definition] Iterative design is repeated cycles of prototyping, testing, analyzing and refining, in which the design is shaped by how players experience it. (x2)
- [definition] A game is functional when a person who knows nothing about it can play from start to finish without the designer's help; it may still be clunky and unsatisfying.
- [definition] RITE, Rapid Iterative Testing and Evaluation, tests a narrow element with one participant at a time and fixes discovered issues before the next participant tests the revised version. When: Iterating quickly on one feature. Why: Fast fixes between sessions speed up improvement.
- [definition] Design thinking finds the right problem, weighs several solutions, and chooses the most suitable one, which is often imperfect.
- [definition] Evaluation asks whether an insight is valuable and original.
- [definition] Elaboration is usually the longest and hardest stage, and much of invention is sustained effort rather than inspiration.
- [definition] Development means financing a team and managing it to deliver a high-quality game on time and within budget.
- [definition] Therapeutic planning is planning mainly to feel better about an uncertain future rather than to coordinate work.
- [definition] Debate exists to find flaws in ideas, exposing hidden assumptions and logical fallacies.
- [definition] The designer owns the player experience from start to finish, making sure mechanics function at every level and working with almost every team member. Why: Gameplay depends on how it is programmed, visualized, and supported by sound and voice.
- [definition] Explicit futures let players trade a future favor for a present resource.
- [definition] Finishing means reaching the single stated long-term goal, while completing means finishing all tasks, including optional quests and locations.
- [definition] Foundational technology enables an experience that was not possible before; decorational technology enhances one that already exists.
- [definition] A transformation check asks how a game can change players for better or worse, since games change people through the experiences they create.
- [definition] Transformational games aim to change players in a specific way, and the term is preferred over serious games, which implies seriousness matters more than fun.
- [definition] A design knob is a tunable number on a game object that can be changed to adjust how the object plays.
- [definition] A transition is an arrow between states, labeled with the condition, such as a die roll or fixed requirement, that allows the change.
- [definition] Feature design is proposing a focused addition to an existing game rather than a whole new game.
- [definition] The creative core, or X, is a concise concept that serves as both a razor for cutting features and a slogan that conveys the game.
- [definition] Game development is deciding which appealing features can be built within limited time and limited cash.
- [definition] Under the tool metaphor, PCG is an instrument that extends a designer's capabilities by adding a generative component to the workflow.
- [definition] The crazy method calls the player's bluff by being unfair, arbitrary, or beyond technical norms, showing that the designer's rules do not follow convention.
- [definition] Design effects are how a decision changes what players experience, and predicting them is the main work of game design.
- [definition] Flow charts map the decisions a player faces as abstract nodes and branches, approximating what the player is thinking rather than physical level geometry.
- [definition] Degenerative design breaks a game's established rules to surprise players, and designers can plan such breaks so the game's logic stays intact.
- [mechanism] As production advances, playtest-revise cycles tighten, so the issues found and the changes made become smaller. Why: Because the game must eventually ship, late iterations are kept to small fixes rather than fundamental redesign.
- [mechanism] When a disruptive technology becomes good enough, being faster, better and cheaper, customers can switch to it almost overnight. When: the disruptive technology reaches a good-enough threshold. Why: Buyers judge the newcomer against today's standards and stay put until it clears a threshold, while it keeps improving unnoticed.
- [mechanism] Short iterations followed by review force strong team communication and build bonds among members. When: Small cross-functional teams working in short, reviewed iterations.
- [mechanism] Going into detail forces the team to think the game through from high-level vision down to file types and font sizes, exposing gaps early. When: When writing a design document that covers the whole game in detail. Why: Conferring on details across text, visuals and specs makes each member check the whole game, catching mismatches before separate work is integrated.
- [mechanism] Demanding perfection at every stage stretches each loop through overanalysis, which means fewer cycles and a weaker final game. When: when each stage is held to near-final quality. Why: Game quality depends most on how many iteration loops occur, and perfectionism lengthens each loop and reduces how many fit in.
- [mechanism] Iterative processes avoid long-range assumptions and run constant reality checks, so disasters tend to surface early. Why: Frequent checks against reality surface unforeseen problems early, and not committing to distant-future assumptions lets the team redirect quickly.
- [mechanism] The main cost of a critical failure is the disruption it causes to other processes, not the fix itself, and damage is worst when it hits during a time-critical event. When: When a hidden flaw surfaces late in development. Why: Failures cascade into blocked work for everyone depending on the broken part.
- [behavior] Designers focus on current problems and ignore potential ones, because existing problems carry emotional weight while potential problems stay abstract. Why: Current problems are cognitively easier to think about than the many new problems a fix might cause.
- [behavior] Design churn comes less from uncertainty in individual pieces than from change shockwaves propagating through dependencies, so concepts can persist while specifics change repeatedly. When: Explaining repeated redesign of upper-layer features. Why: Changes in foundations force revisions upward through every dependent system.
- [behavior] Players expect novelty and careful balance, so designers must iterate constantly, and waiting on engineers or slow recompiles breaks creative flow. When: Planning production workflows for designer-facing behavior. Why: Constant iteration means long turnaround on each change interrupts design momentum.
- [behavior] Designers often understand the case for iteration intellectually but only come to believe it emotionally after painful planning failures on a complete, significant game. When: When a designer has read about iteration but still plans deeply. Why: Belief depends on felt consequences, not on abstract argument.
- [behavior] Strong designers signal uncertainty with phrases such as 'my gut says' or 'the way I see it', which keeps discussion open. When: When a designer proposes ideas in a team discussion that could turn into disagreement. Why: Such phrasing reflects that all knowledge is uncertain and leaves room for others' better ideas.
- [rule] Fail early and often, learning from each failure, so that success arrives sooner. When: Iterating on design and prototypes. Why: Failures before large investment pay off, and rapid failure yields information faster than extended planning.
- [rule] At foundation, playtest alone to confirm the core idea is fun; defer loopholes, dead ends, and questions like movement range, interaction, and conflict resolution. When: Testing a bare core-mechanic prototype during foundation prototyping. Why: Answering every question early slows progress; answers emerge through play.
- [rule] Use the framework's pillars to structure team discussions and to organize research and analytics questions. Why: A single shared framework gives teams a common structure across design, research, and analytics work.
- [rule] Divide the design problem into small, manageable pieces, since building every system at once amounts to building the full game and tends to fail. Why: Building every system at once prevents fast work and reaching any clear conclusion, so the prototype fails.
- [rule] The foundation stage goal is to make the game work, even as a rough approximation. When: Early prototype work, while experimenting with the fundamental concepts of the system.
- [rule] Begin formal detailing only after the foundation shows promise and the structure lets others play the game. Why: A foundation-only build only tests the designer's own intuition, so others need enough structure to play it.
- [rule] Make only one change per iteration and retest the whole system afterward to gauge its effects. Why: A single change touches one aspect of gameplay, so balancing becomes a methodical process rather than guesswork.
- [rule] Check each refinement change against the experience originally intended, since changes can drift from the vision.
- [rule] The highest priority is keeping clients happy by shipping useful software early and on an ongoing basis. When: when planning a development project.
- [rule] Treat ideas as cheap and disposable, and reverse an early decision as soon as evidence shows it is not working. When: when an early choice starts failing. Why: Attachment to a decision keeps teams committed to designs that are clearly failing.
- [rule] Make every design loop count and loop as fast as possible, because loops are expensive for large games. When: when a game needs long test-and-improve cycles. Why: Large computer games can afford few loops, so each one must produce useful information.
- [rule] No decision is final until the whole team agrees it is final. When: When a meeting participant seems unconvinced despite verbally agreeing. Why: Unresolved doubt can turn into quiet dissent, and the team loses contributions that person would have made.
- [rule] Do not wait for a template before starting to design; begin recording ideas now. When: Before a design template is available.
- [rule] Know the design thoroughly and have at least a reasoned guess for unsettled parts, including play hours, time to finish a level, and how multiplayer works. When: Preparing for a pitch. Why: Listeners probe design details, and gaps reduce confidence.
- [rule] Do important self-reflection now rather than postponing it, since time runs out without warning. Why: Life and career are short.
- [rule] Identify your personal theme as early as possible in a project or career. Why: Knowing your personal purpose triggers a significant shift in how your creative thinking works.
- [rule] Discuss ideas as a conversation rather than an argument. When: Disagreeing with team members about design. Why: Teams working long hours depend on members who communicate and get along, which matter more than tool skills that change often.
- [rule] In the earliest development stages, make core moment-to-moment gameplay fun first, since balance is not yet meaningful while mechanics keep shifting. When: earliest development stages. Why: Mechanics shift constantly at this stage.
- [rule] Enrich the game UX framework with academic research and the game UX community's work rather than treating it as fixed. Why: The framework grew from industry practice and has not been tested academically, so it stays open to revision.
- [rule] Draw the design document's table of contents from the prototype, flowchart, and wireframes. Why: Writing from the game's own artifacts lets the document unfold in stages, rather than requiring the whole game to be worked out at once.
- [rule] Decide early who will engage with the product, what they will spend on it, and why, and choose the target market, since casual and hardcore markets need quite different approaches. Why: Hardcore players spend more on games they care about, while casual players are hard to get noticed by.
- [rule] A design is finished only when it passes all eight filters without further change; filters may be adjusted when constraints demand, such as retargeting a different demographic. When: when reviewing a design before committing time, or when project constraints force change. Why: A fix that passes one filter can break another, so every change must be rechecked against all the filters.
- [rule] Before revising, duplicate the source document as a fresh draft labeled with its filename and date, enabling reversion to the prior version. When: before starting a revision pass. Why: Allows reversing edits that fail to pan out.
- [rule] Plan only the current loop in detail, since there is no need to predict events deep into the future. When: during iterative development. Why: Each test checks assumptions against reality, giving reliable knowledge to base the next loop's plan on.
- [rule] Apply iteration loops to a whole game or to smaller parts such as a level, tool, or interface, and run many loops in parallel on large teams.
- [rule] Keep the design fluid rather than locking in unchangeable decisions, and check dependency-driven risk every day. When: When business or marketing want a design decision long before the game needs it. Why: Detailed decisions high in the dependency stack are likely to be reshaped by cascading changes, so early commitment is risky.
- [rule] A finished game should be functional, complete, balanced, fun, and accessible.
- [rule] Understand a game's internal anatomy, not just its surface, so you can find the true cause of a problem and choose the best fix. When: When a game is failing or being revised. Why: A fix aimed at the wrong cause can leave the game failing.
- [rule] To bring learners into readiness, paint concrete scenarios and pose problems that make the information matter. When: teaching new knowledge. Why: Concrete problems create the state in which learners absorb information.
- [rule] Ask both how the game can change players for the better and how it can change them for the worse. When: during game design. Why: The same design can produce either outcome.
- [rule] Describe why a feature matters from the player's view, such as being left barehanded against monsters without a crafted weapon. When: When specifying a feature that players must learn to use. Why: Framing the feature in the player's own terms creates meaningful situations in which to teach it.
- [rule] Players should be informed when advanced AI, including generative models, is used, and such systems must be safe.
- [rule] Treat framework terms as a launch pad rather than a limit, and move beyond them as design experience grows. When: after gaining design experience. Why: A framework structures design work but should not restrict the designer.
- [rule] Generate conflict via regulations, routines, and circumstances like competitive multiplayer matches.
- [rule] Design dungeons from the intended gameplay experience outward to the space, not from a complex space toward the experience. Why: Dungeon exploration stays tightly tied to controlled progression of challenges, rewards and pacing, which the space must serve.
- [rule] Choose designs that fit the content already built, instead of making the rest of the game bend to them. Why: Containing a design's side effects across finished levels costs more and thins the fiction.
- [rule] Build the core first, then draw features from the design backlog and iterate outward from the core. Why: Everything else depends on the core mechanics, so finishing them early gives a testable base for iteration.
- [rule] Use differently shaped nodes for different node types and keep the visual language consistent; keep node content to short text. When: drawing flow charts. Why: Consistent shapes let a reader tell event types apart at a glance.
- [heuristic] Good design comes from understanding what you have built and improving it step by step over months or years, not from a single flash of brilliance. Why: Listening to playtesters shows what the mechanics actually do, not what the designer wants or imagines they should do.
- [heuristic] The iteration loop is a good basic approach but is not sufficient on its own. Why: Design knowledge comes from several methods with different costs, and the team must react to changing project conditions.
- [heuristic] If playtests show a mechanic works better reversed, change the story to support the reversed version. Why: A story change can make a mechanic believable, so the team does not have to discard a mechanic that playtests favoured.
- [heuristic] Do not agonize over the first iteration of numbers; every designer's first numbers will be off. When: Early numeric design. Why: Balance is an ongoing process and player preferences shift, so hitting the target on the first attempt is unlikely.
- [heuristic] Time to figure out a task is more informative than success or failure, since think-aloud changes how long players take to understand. When: Measuring think-aloud results. Why: Speaking changes the task, so duration reveals more than outcome.
- [heuristic] Keep adding, testing and removing optional rules, since each addition can spark new design ideas.
- [heuristic] Early definitions can be revised later, but they give the team a framework that prevents aimless trial and error during production. When: Early in development, before a production team is launched. Why: Physical prototyping forces the designer to define each element and how the elements interact before any digital build exists.
- [heuristic] Minimize the time to a prototype's first failure, meaning its first rejected hypothesis, and be willing to stop early once it occurs. Why: Checking a prototype against its hypothesis is scientific practice, and an early failure avoids building ideas the designer does not actually have.
- [heuristic] Single-change discipline matters most in late fine-tuning, when many playtester requests arrive at once and tempt batching. When: Late fine-tuning. Why: Changing many things at once can make balancing uncontrollable and lose parts of the design that already worked.
- [heuristic] Each design loop sharpens the problem statement, and early prototypes expose serious problems while they are cheap to fix. Why: Even a little playtesting reveals requirements such as networked play and which visual effects are too costly.
- [heuristic] The number of loops needed to resolve a design cannot be planned in advance.
- [heuristic] Simple card or board games can be built and tweaked repeatedly rather than following a strict loop-count plan. When: when the game is cheap to build and change. Why: A simple game can be prototyped in an hour or two, so repeated trial and error costs little.
- [heuristic] Loop as fast as possible by building many rough prototypes rather than polished ones. When: Iterating on design questions during development. Why: Rough prototypes are cheap, so more loops fit into the available time.
- [heuristic] With online distribution, the iteration loop continues long after launch, so balancing never fully ends. When: Games distributed online and updated after release. Why: Patches and value changes can be delivered after the game ships.
- [heuristic] If you feel pulled toward work that connects to no purpose you can name, question whether to pursue it. Why: Only the designer can identify the personal purpose that makes the work worth doing.
- [heuristic] Some inconsistencies are unavoidable in production, and awareness of them helps reduce their impact. Why: Each feel element shapes how players perceive the game's physical reality, so awareness lets you anticipate where small annoyances will show.
- [heuristic] Rank issues by severity to players and by importance of the feature affected; a core-pillar issue can outrank a more severe minor one. Why: Priority depends on how badly players are hurt and how central the affected feature is, so a mild hit to a pillar can matter more.
- [heuristic] Iterative design should include checking whether players are actually having fun. Why: Fun is hard to assess and personal to each player, so testing is how you confirm it is actually there.
- [heuristic] Shared frameworks keep discussions about fun productive and prevent endless debate when fun is subjective.
- [heuristic] Ideas are cheap; execution matters more, and a well-matured average idea can beat a poorly implemented great one. Why: What matters is how the end user experiences the product, not what the creators had in mind.
- [heuristic] Discussing high-level goals while facing constraints, and staying concrete while dreaming, unifies design work and keeps it deliverable. When: When moving between high-level vision and real-world production constraints. Why: Dreams must accept limits to be produced, so keeping both levels in view ties vision to what can actually ship.
- [heuristic] Make training fun, since fun both drives productivity and reflects it. When: When running short workshops that teach teams new tools. Why: Fun makes people more creative, receptive and productive, and it may both cause and reflect productivity.
- [heuristic] Speeding up a fun game is simpler than making a fast game entertaining. When: prioritizing early game development. Why: Gameplay design is harder to retrofit onto optimized code than performance is to add later.
- [heuristic] Give level editors undo, because designers become frustrated when they cannot reverse edits. When: building game levels with an editor tool.
- [heuristic] Step away from the script for a day, a weekend, or a week before a full revision, to gain distance and spot mistakes you could not see while writing. When: before a full revision. Why: Being immersed mid-draft hides errors, and time away lets the writer evaluate the work with fresh eyes.
- [heuristic] Each repeated revision pass usually needs fewer rewrites than the pass before it. When: repeating revision cycles. Why: Rewriting a scene restarts it as a first draft, so keeping existing work keeps each draft tight.
- [heuristic] Use the interaction-count check early as a rule of thumb; this judgment improves with experience and is not a substitute for testing. When: evaluating designs before implementation. Why: Heavy testing takes a lot of time, so designers need ways to spot elegance on paper first.
- [heuristic] Expect progress in small increments over years rather than a single breakthrough; no one moment marks the work as complete. Why: Each iteration fixed the feeblest parts, drew lessons from breakdowns, and built on what worked.
- [heuristic] Make future deals explicit when players strike many complex, ongoing deals; skip it when deals are simple enough to remember. When: When the game encourages many complex ongoing deals between players. Why: Players forget the details of many complex deals, so the game should record them; simple deals are remembered by players.
- [heuristic] When player-written FAQs make a game noticeably better, the game is likely starved of information. Why: Without understanding a choice's implications, players cannot plan, so the game's value stays hidden until they learn it.
- [heuristic] Each outside field supports a design task: economics informs in-game resource economies, and mathematics covers probability, risk, and scoring systems. When: Choosing which outside knowledge to study. Why: Matching fields to concrete tasks shows where they are useful.
- [heuristic] To test for the major gift, start designing and notice whether the work makes you happy; early confidence does not settle it. When: Deciding whether design suits you. Why: Enjoyment shows only once you actually design.
- [heuristic] A mechanics taxonomy should aim to produce great games rather than philosophical completeness, accepting some gray areas for practical use. When: when classifying a game's mechanics. Why: Designers need knowledge that serves making great games, and philosophically watertight analysis is less useful to them.
- [heuristic] In a fair seeding, each lower rank has a lower title chance than the rank above it; if a lower seed has an easier path, the seeding is unfair. When: when comparing candidate bracket seedings. Why: A lower seed's path can contain more stronger opponents than a higher seed's path, changing its title chance.
- [heuristic] Higher visual realism demands more detailed control, simulation and effects, since players notice inconsistencies more. When: Increasing rendering realism. Why: A realistic treatment creates expectations about behavior, while an iconic, absurd one sets few, so the detail must match.
- [heuristic] A fascination with a subject, such as social insects, seen as a system to study and deconstruct, can generate original game concepts. When: when seeking original concepts from the world. Why: Viewing a subject as a system with rules and competition gives designers organized structure to turn into play.
- [heuristic] Modifying existing genre mechanics attracts core genre players because it appeals to their taste for novelty while keeping familiar play. When: When your idea fits an established genre and its audience. Why: Core genre players receive novelty layered onto the play they already know, so they are likely to engage.
- [heuristic] The future of game design depends more on serving deeper play and interaction than on immersive devices. Why: Immersive technology matters only insofar as it serves deeper play, which is what lets games move people beyond traditional media.
- [heuristic] The greatest competitive win is a value no other game offers, since such a value attracts players who have nowhere else to go. When: When looking for a distinctive design position. Why: Players seeking an unserved experience have no alternatives and must choose the game.
- [heuristic] Flow charts suit branching stories and complex systems, and also work for mapping key gameplay points in a level. When: choosing a diagram for branching or systemic design. Why: They model the sequence of decisions a player moves through, mirroring the player's reasoning rather than the physical layout of space.
- [heuristic] Define the core experience first, then extend the design beyond its edges so important content is not lost at the boundaries. When: when planning the scope of an experience with no hard cutoff. Why: Audience context blurs where the experience starts and ends, so content placed exactly at the edge gets cut.
- [procedure] 1) Observe players. 2) Generate ideas. 3) Prototype and try it out. 4) Test and revise. 5) Repeat until the design is satisfying, or good enough. (x2)
- [procedure] 1) Make the game functional. 2) Make it internally complete and balanced. 3) Refine it only after that, restoring and strengthening the originally envisioned fun. When: ordering design work.
- [procedure] Run prototypes concurrently by discipline: engineers test technology, artists test art, and designers test gameplay at once, using many small independent prototypes. When: Multiple team members are available and several questions are open at once. Why: Parallel independent prototypes answer more questions in less elapsed time than a single sequential prototype, multiplying the design loops completed in a given time.
- [procedure] Discover deep object interactions by repeated playtesting, since they rarely appear in design documents. Why: Unhandled object uses break presence and remind players they are playing a game, so these interactions matter.
- [procedure] 1) Build an analytics plan listing key questions and what good answers look like. 2) Run the playtest. 3) Note each problem. 4) Modify game objects or fix the cost curve and its math. 5) Repeat as time allows. When: balancing a game through repeated playtests. Why: The goal is balance as close to perfect as time allows.
- [procedure] 1) Observe players during the session. 2) Ask them questions. 3) Adjust the design based on what you saw. 4) Test again. When: after each playtest session. Why: Player experience cannot be fully predicted, so decisions must rest on how the prototype actually plays for testers.
- [procedure] 1) Modify one element of a working prototype, such as its board, units, goal or play procedures. 2) Change one aspect at a time so its effect is identifiable. 3) Play the variant with a partner. 4) Describe the change's effect on play in writing. 5) Note any new system that emerges and consider spinning it off. Why: Manipulating the structure of a prototype invariably sparks new ideas, and entirely new systems can emerge from it.
- [procedure] Iterative design cycle: 1) Produce a prototype. 2) Test whether a user can interact with it properly. 3) Evaluate the results. 4) Change the design. 5) Test again. When: during any stage of development. Why: each loop tightens the design against real use.
- [procedure] 1) Test the design against all eight filters. 2) If any filter fails, change the design. 3) Rerun all eight filters on the revised design. When: After a design revision made to fix a failed filter. Why: Fixing one failed filter can break a filter the design already passed, so the full set must be rechecked.
- [procedure] 1) Test the narrow element with one participant. 2) Fix the issues found. 3) Test the revised prototype with the next participant. 4) Repeat. When: Running RITE on a single feature. Why: Each session tests the latest fixes.
- [procedure] 1) Analyze the formal system: part sizes and counts, starting positions, and purpose of special areas. 2) Decide whether skill or chance decides outcomes. 3) Set a player experience goal. 4) Brainstorm several goals before changing anything. 5) Choose a metaphor matching the goal. 6) Modify or rebuild the prototype. 7) Playtest and check whether the goal was met. When: When modifying an existing prototype's system. Why: Changes should serve a chosen experience goal rather than change for its own sake.
- [procedure] 1) Find a loophole or dead end. 2) Tweak the system. 3) Playtest again. 4) Repeat until no critical deficiencies remain. When: Fixing loopholes and dead ends. Why: Each fix can take days or weeks.
- [procedure] 1) Write several original solutions to a design problem. 2) List the pros and cons of each solution.
- [procedure] 1) Run the game. 2) Navigate to the test spot. 3) Test. 4) Write or change code while the game keeps running. 5) Return to testing without restarting. When: when the scripting system supports code changes during play. Why: Avoids the compile, link, and restart cost of each cycle, allowing more loops per day.
- [procedure] 1) Write code. 2) Compile and link. 3) Run the game. 4) Navigate to the test spot. 5) Test. 6) Restart the cycle if the result is bad. When: when changes require a full rebuild before testing.
- [procedure] 1) State the problem. 2) Brainstorm possible solutions. 3) Choose a solution. 4) List the risks of that solution. 5) Build prototypes to mitigate those risks. 6) Test the prototypes; stop if good enough. 7) State the new problems and return to step 2. When: when iterating on a design problem. Why: Each round makes the problem statement more specific.
- [procedure] 1) If results show a fundamentally flawed idea, return to step one. 2) If results point to improvements, modify and retest. 3) If positive, the loop is complete. Without funding at step three, return to step one. When: after each playtest in the playcentric process.
- [procedure] 1) Each person writes an article and an adjective, folds the paper, and passes it on. 2) Each then adds a noun, a verb, an article and adjective, and a noun. 3) Unfold the paper and read the sentences aloud. Why: Chance combined with the unconscious mind produces unexpected ideas that deliberate brainstorming tends to miss.
- [procedure] 1) Give each team a key feature to test. 2) Allow 20 minutes to build a physical prototype of it. 3) Play and compare results with the team. When: Run as a short exercise when teams rarely build physical prototypes. Why: Time limits force quick, cheap builds whose results are often surprising.
- [procedure] 1) Pick one challenge. 2) Set a 60-minute limit. 3) Aim for 100 ideas. 4) Number ideas as they are written. 5) Keep energy high throughout. When: When running a rapid brainstorm. Why: A high quantity target pushes past obvious ideas.
- [procedure] 1) Finish every revision step. 2) Take a break. 3) Run the whole checklist again. 4) Repeat until the script is polished enough for the lead, editor, or producer to review. When: after each full pass. Why: Each repeated pass tends to need fewer rewrites than the one before, so the script converges.
- [procedure] 1) Sketch and prototype designs guided by HCI principles. 2) Gather user feedback on the prototype. 3) Redefine the design from that feedback. 4) Test again, repeating until the target is met or time or budget runs out. Why: Perception is subjective, and the experience lives in people's minds rather than in the design itself.
- [procedure] 1) Fix the number of loops in advance, or 2) loop until quality is acceptable or budget runs out. When: when deciding how long to keep iterating. Why: Each test checks assumptions against reality, so long-range plans beyond the current loop are unnecessary.
- [procedure] 1) State the design problem. 2) Generate an initial idea. 3) Push the idea through all eight filters, revising until it passes. When: When a design fails one of the eight filters during revision. Why: Fixing one failed filter can break a filter the design already passed, so the full set must be rechecked.
- [procedure] 1) Propose a definition. 2) Find fun things that lack pleasure. 3) Find pleasurable things with surprises that are not fun. 4) Revise the definition to fit both kinds of case. When: when defining a design term. Why: Counterexamples expose cases a proposed definition misses, showing where it needs revising.
- [procedure] 1) State your theme clearly enough to guide decisions. 2) Check whether every available means reinforces that theme. 3) Separate the elements with the elemental tetrad and test each against the theme. When: When checking whether a game's parts serve one central theme.
- [procedure] 1) List ten favorite games. 2) Name each game's objective. 3) Look for shared objective types. 4) Define the kinds of games that appeal to you. Why: Seeing which objectives you like or dislike shows which objective types to use in your own ideas.
- [procedure] Before keeping a task, check that it is necessary and that its decision is neither obvious, hollow, nor uninformed. Why: Tasks that are unnecessary or have obvious, hollow, or uninformed decisions can kill the fun of the original concept.
- [tradeoff] Iterative gains climb toward good-enough solutions, but can trap a design on a low hill; reaching a higher peak needs large untested jumps, taken only when refinement stalls. When: when deciding whether small iterative refinements will reach a good design. Why: Small steps cannot cross the valley between a low hill and a higher mountain. (x2)
- [tradeoff] An exhaustive up-front design document rarely matches the final game, whereas iteration is more efficient and robust. Why: Observed playtester responses correct the design as it goes, while a written document fixes assumptions before any play.
- [tradeoff] Retesting the whole system after each change is slow, but batching several changes saves time at the cost of unclear cause and effect. Why: Changing one element at a time isolates each effect to one aspect, so the cause of a result stays identifiable.
- [tradeoff] One-liner descriptions suit internal use, while comparisons suit outside gatekeepers who need market, gameplay, audience and similar-game information. When: Choosing how to present an idea to a given audience. Why: Each format gives different information.
- [tradeoff] Revisions made to cut cost, ease production or support marketing can lower the quality of the narrative. Why: Rewriting dialogue is far cheaper than reanimating the scene it accompanies, so text is revised for savings.
- [tradeoff] Committing early to a detailed, high-stack decision may cost more in rework risk than its business or marketing benefit is worth. When: When marketing or business needs a high-stack, detailed design decision before the game is ready. Why: Decisions high in the dependency stack get reshaped by cascading changes to lower layers, so early commitment is likely to be reworked.
- [tradeoff] Characters may share one slot layout, or classes may differ in slot count and in which item types each slot accepts. When: designing character equipment. Why: Differing slots add class identity but increase balance work.
- [tradeoff] Time, budget and technical capacity are limited, so a new feature competes with fixing existing UX issues. When: when a proposal would take resources from UX fixes. Why: resources are finite.
- [pitfall] Cloning the biggest hit with rushed, budget-cut copies saturates the niche and lowers production values; adding features without removing anything leaves no value above competitors and predictable market failure. When: When building a clone of a successful game. Why: A copy cannot be the original, so every other version looks weaker beside it, and lower sales push studios to cut costs further. (x2)
- [pitfall] Many games are not thoroughly prototyped, so they fail to reach their full potential. When: When core mechanics go untested until late in production. Why: Flaws surface late, when they may be too costly or impossible to fix.
- [pitfall] Letting the design get ahead of iterative research removes room for change, and some assumptions will prove wrong. Why: Assumptions are often wrong, so the design needs room to change based on playtest learning.
- [pitfall] Iterating in any order other than dependency order wastes much of the benefit of iteration. Why: Each change sends ripples through dependencies, so iteration must follow those links.
- [pitfall] Designing AI around a fixed set of game inputs leads to rework late in the project.
- [pitfall] Small incremental steps can trap a designer on a local maximum that only a larger redesign would escape. Why: Incremental changes can only reach nearby improvements, while a better design may need a fundamental change.
- [pitfall] No save design is perfect; if the designer does not decide, the gameplay programmer will decide by what is easiest to code. Why: Leaving it unchosen means the implementer picks whatever is simplest to build, not what suits play.
- [pitfall] Depth cannot be reliably predicted during design, so it must be tested with players rather than assumed. Why: Which element combinations produce depth cannot be foreseen in advance.
- [pitfall] Assuming any single enjoyment or fun framework is empirically proven leads to overconfidence in its predictions. Why: Many fun frameworks are hard to unify, and some lack empirical validation.
- [pitfall] Designers too close to their work may fix one problem while creating several others, and keep changing the game until it loses its appeal. When: Especially harmful in a project's final development phases. Why: Lack of outside perspective and repeated unchecked changes.
- [pitfall] Starting a prototype without a stated purpose produces results that answer nothing specific.
- [pitfall] Expecting high-level work early is unrealistic. It is not the norm, though it can happen. When: When someone starts a writing career at entry level on a team. Why: Core creative work is usually reserved for leads and executives, so juniors rarely get it.
- [pitfall] A design document specifying everything in advance can contradict real play; building strictly from it wastes effort, and trusting it over observed play leads to ignoring discoveries or discarding it. When: when a full design document is treated as fixed and observed play contradicts it. Why: The document fixes every piece in advance, so pieces are placed as predetermined rather than as play reveals.
- [pitfall] Early negative focus-group results and executive doubt can wrongly kill an unconventional concept, so a skunkworks effort can keep it alive until it proves itself. When: When testing an unfamiliar concept with a small group. Why: Unfamiliar concepts often test badly because audiences and executives judge them against familiar categories.
- [pitfall] Treating the current best standard as the ceiling on what is achievable limits progress. Why: People do only what a goal requires, so once they top the standard, they stop.
- [pitfall] Using existing games as the main reference from the start makes the new design derivative. When: When a design is described without first physically prototyping it. Why: Without physical prototyping, the design description leans on existing games, binding it to them.
- [pitfall] Decorative technology cannot rescue a boring game; a novel feature added to weak core gameplay will still flop. Why: Novel features do not compensate for weak core play; they cannot carry a game that is dull at its center.
- [pitfall] Accepting a professional obligation without thinking it through is risky, because a commitment like this is hard to set aside once made. When: When deciding whether to accept the responsibilities of a game designer. Why: The obligation is meant to be lifelong, serving as a constant reminder of responsibility.
- [pitfall] Many submitted games are rejected because they are not fun to play. When: When submitting a game concept to publishers.
- [fact] Game design is at once an individual craft and a shared, collaborative conversation between designers and players.
- [fact] Iterative design comes from human-computer interface design and resembles the scientific method, repeatedly testing a hypothesis.
- [fact] The usability and engageability framework can guide collaboration across teams, iterative design, user research and analytics.
- [fact] The formal details stage can make or break a game.
- [fact] Iteration loops vary by project: narrative games iterate story beats first, and loops may involve one person or ten over weeks.
- [fact] In the trough of disillusionment a technology fails to meet its hype and can become unfashionable or despised.
- [fact] Expansions may introduce entirely new mechanics, and their development follows a process similar to the original game's, usually on a shorter timeline.
- [fact] Many structures and ideas from one driving implementation generalize to other driving mechanics, even when specific implementations differ.
- [fact] The game UX framework was built from team-level practice and has not yet been tested academically.
- [fact] Fun rating scales are not a good predictor of a game's later success or enjoyment.
- [fact] User research helps teams identify early which problems to solve, rank their severity, and find solutions.
- [fact] A game degree helps but is not required; many successful developers never earned one.
- [fact] Understanding, changing and cleaning up code is an ongoing loop that never truly ends.
- [fact] Revision checks cover the outline, wireframe, cuts, character consistency, and player agency, and should be repeated after each revision pass. When: during any revision.
- [fact] A design framework lists fifteen properties shared by things that feel alive; the first five are levels of scale, strong centers, boundaries, alternating repetition and positive space.
- [fact] No standard notation exists for foundational rules, so designers usually write them down only as needed.
- [fact] Games have different target lengths, ranging from about 3 minutes for an arcade game to about 100 hours for a CRPG, and progression can be tuned to keep play within that range.
- [fact] A designer's plan becomes an interactive experience only when players take part, much as a blueprint or script waits for use.

## ideation
- [model] Creativity is described as five stages: preparation, incubation, insight, evaluation, and elaboration. Why: Insight timing cannot be predicted, so preparation and incubation need constant attention.
- [definition] Procedural literacy covers knowing how machines operate, how code is organized, and the broad principles of game programming.
- [definition] A blue sky project assumes a technology is possible even though it cannot be built today, so ideas are not limited by current capability.
- [definition] Incubation is a period when ideas churn below conscious awareness; it can last years or only a few hours.
- [definition] Insight is the 'aha' moment when separate pieces come together into an idea.
- [definition] Brainstorming is a semiformal group process for quickly producing large numbers of diverse ideas.
- [definition] The nameless quality is a hard-to-name trait of well-designed things that makes them feel alive, whole, comfortable, free, and free from inner contradictions; people recognize it by experience. When: Evaluating whether a design feels special or lifeless. Why: It resists verbal definition but is recognized in well-made places and objects.
- [mechanism] A change in one part of a game system can alter how players perceive other parts, so fixes produce ripple effects. When: Changing one element of an interrelated system. Why: Game systems are interrelated.
- [mechanism] Silly prompts loosen thinking and reveal new perspectives, and new perspectives tend to produce strong ideas. When: Stuck or rigid brainstorming sessions. Why: Playful framing breaks habitual thinking.
- [mechanism] Crunch, tiredness and stress all reduce performance.
- [mechanism] Rumination forms new connections between old ideas, so a larger store of knowledge yields more possible connections. When: When seeking a creative solution to a design problem. Why: Creative association draws on existing material.
- [mechanism] A group's output is shaped more by how its structure, culture and hierarchy route knowledge, power and resources than by the personalities of its members. When: When a team's structure routes knowledge, power and resources in a poor direction. Why: Capable individuals in a badly routed organization can collectively act foolishly.
- [mechanism] Ants find food through random wandering, then lay pheromone trails on the return trip; other ants follow, and each successful trip reinforces the trail, making rich sources' paths straighter and stronger. When: When the path ends at an abundant food source. Why: Followers reinforce what earlier successes marked, so trail quality compounds without any central control.
- [mechanism] Before speaking up, people weigh credit and learning against wasted time and public rejection; a good climate tips this toward sharing ideas, and a fearful one tips it toward silence. When: When a person decides whether to share an idea with colleagues. Why: People tally expected credit and learning against time wasted and public rejection before deciding to speak.
- [mechanism] Open-ended creation without set goals, and focused on building rather than destroying, can become a breakout genre with a broad audience. When: When a game centers on creating things without set goals. Why: Players are drawn to making things on their own terms.
- [mechanism] Letting outside elements into a game, such as real money or extra cards, turns it into a different game. When: When outside elements such as real money or added cards enter the game's boundary. Why: Game boundaries are what define the game, so outside elements that cross them change it intrinsically.
- [mechanism] Because interactions multiply, a small rule set can generate endless different games, from long struggles to quick sweeps. Why: Interactions among simple mechanics multiply into a large number of possible play experiences.
- [behavior] Designers feel a strong sense of accomplishment when a mechanic finally works, and searching for starvation threatens that feeling, so they may avoid testing.
- [behavior] Playing a real, even crude, build produces concrete ideas that come while inspiration is fresh, which thinking on paper rarely yields. When: Early prototyping of a concept. Why: Concrete play experience exposes problems and possibilities imagination cannot reach.
- [behavior] An odd idea can be explored by extending it until it becomes a distinct concept, even if it is later rejected. When: when a strange idea surfaces during brainstorming. Why: exploration turns a random thought into a usable concept.
- [behavior] Most people who offer design ideas want to be heard, not to take control of the design. Why: Their motive is caring about the outcome, which is why being heard satisfies them rather than taking over.
- [behavior] People tend to jump to solutions before they have clearly defined the problem. Why: Rapid mental processing favors immediate fixes over careful problem framing.
- [behavior] Unconscious rumination keeps working on a problem after conscious effort stops, so surprising insights can arrive during idle time with unpredictable timing. When: Taking a break from direct testing of a problem. Why: The mind continues connecting ideas during unrelated activity.
- [behavior] Rereading your own script after a break often surprises you with your earlier choices, and this reaction is normal. When: rereading a script after a break. Why: Distance from the writing process.
- [behavior] Quick, low-effort handling of unimportant decisions can look like laziness but is rational indifference, since the decision barely affects the game. When: When a minor design decision has no significant effect. Why: Spending heavy effort on trivial choices wastes time better used elsewhere.
- [behavior] Going along with the group takes little thought and avoids social risk, but flaws go unnoticed and spread through the design. Why: Constant agreement signals laziness or fear, so no one is left to flag inconsistencies.
- [rule] While generating ideas, do not self-censor when working alone; in a team, do not censor, dismiss or criticize others' ideas before they are developed. When: during idea generation. Why: Criticizing ideas too early breaks the flow of open thinking and collaborative extension that brainstorming depends on. (x2)
- [rule] Record ideas constantly in a notebook, handheld device, cheap voice recorder or app, and transcribe periodically before they fade. When: throughout the creative process. Why: Human memory is poor, and unrecorded ideas keep occupying mental energy until recorded, freeing the mind to relax. (x2)
- [rule] Look for design inspiration outside games and outside your own genre; cultivate unusual interests to escape the echo chamber and gain distinct design material. Why: Unusual sources supply ideas peers do not share. (x2)
- [rule] Write down everything related to the problem, including silly ideas, since a silly idea can inspire a good one; memory is poor and combining ideas requires paper. Why: Writing also clears the mind of crowded ideas.
- [rule] Use several brainstorming structures and vary them from project to project, letting leaders try unfamiliar ones and inviting members to suggest and lead sessions, and develop your own. When: when the usual structure stops producing ideas. Why: Each designer and game needs a different process, since fixed processes tend to produce the same results.
- [rule] State the emotional response you want each asset to produce, not only what it depicts. When: When coordinating with other teams on environmental narrative assets.
- [rule] Start designing games now instead of waiting for permission to become a designer. When: Deciding how to begin a career in game design. Why: Designers become designers by doing the work, not by waiting for approval.
- [rule] Immerse yourself in the problem: observe your audience in real settings, learn your technology deeply, study adaptations of your story, and play games with and without your core mechanic. When: Preparing for brainstorming on a game concept. Why: Deep exposure supplies material for new ideas.
- [rule] Use the rational mind for planning, taking precautions, and practical matters such as paying taxes. When: When planning, taking precautions, or handling practical matters instead of brainstorming. Why: The rational side is needed to plan and set things straight, which the impulsive subconscious cannot do alone.
- [rule] After brainstorming, narrow the broad list of ideas and develop the best ones into something useful. When: After a brainstorming session has produced a long list of raw ideas.
- [rule] Take odd thoughts, feelings, and dreams seriously and reflect on them briefly, since the subconscious offers more when its ideas are valued. When: during brainstorming or idle moments. Why: serious consideration encourages the subconscious to keep contributing.
- [rule] Limit brainstorming groups to four people or fewer, since adding more people does not necessarily help. When: when forming a brainstorming session.
- [rule] Maximize the work not done, and have the team regularly reflect and adjust its behavior. When: when planning and running development. Why: Regular reflection lets the team become more effective and adjust its behavior over time.
- [rule] Before choosing a seed idea, consider what it must grow into to survive later evaluation. When: when selecting a seed idea.
- [rule] Judge ideas by how well they solve the stated problem rather than by personal preference, using the problem statement as the anchor. When: When team members discuss and evaluate design ideas. Why: Without a problem anchor, discussion becomes a contest of opinions and gut feelings rather than of solutions.
- [rule] Refer to ideas by their content rather than their owner, so the whole team takes ownership of them.
- [rule] Before brainstorming, state a precise challenge, ideally as a player experience goal, and set aside solutions you already have in mind. When: at the start of each session. Why: Paring a challenge down to its core frees the mind from solutions already in view.
- [rule] Open contributions with an affirming 'yes, and' response so that each idea builds on the previous one. When: During a group brainstorm, while colleagues' ideas are still undeveloped. Why: Criticism of half-formed ideas breaks the momentum and makes people hold back, which stifles creative output.
- [rule] Hold brainstorms in a neutral space away from normal desks, and use simple playful objects to help people relax. When: When the usual desk-and-screen work area makes it hard to think creatively. Why: Familiar work settings resist free thinking, while playful distractions help the group relax and think more openly.
- [rule] Post ideas on whiteboards or large taped paper so the whole group can see and build on them; this gets people standing and talking. Why: Ideas the whole group can see spark further ideas and support collaboration.
- [rule] Number each idea so the group can cross-reference during a session and track output. When: When developing a big concept with many linked ideas. Why: Numbers let the group cross-reference ideas by shorthand without losing the larger line of thought, and they track output.
- [rule] Develop ideas left over from a brainstorm in the following days rather than extending the session. When: When a brainstorming session has gone on for about an hour. Why: Brainstorming is high-energy, so mental and physical fatigue sets in after roughly an hour of focused work.
- [rule] When an idea cannot be built yet, brainstorm workarounds or keep it for when technology catches up. When: When an idea is not technically possible yet. Why: Technology may advance later and make the idea feasible, so it is worth keeping rather than discarding.
- [rule] Cut ideas that are too expensive or ambitious unless they can be scaled down, and keep them on a list for a future larger project. When: When an idea exceeds the team's time or budget and cannot be scaled down. Why: Keeping cut ideas on a list preserves them for a later, larger project with more resources.
- [rule] Discuss the top five to ten ideas in a positive tone, weighing each idea's merits rather than bashing it. When: when choosing among shortlisted ideas.
